GEORGE DOUGLAS PAPERS ANNOTATED TRANSCRIPTION
Version 1.1 • September 2026

PURPOSE AND SCOPE
This edition offers a direct, annotated transcription of the six supplied PDFs of the George Douglas Papers, 1830–1939, Center for Restoration Studies MS 5, Abilene Christian University Special Collections and Archives. It accounts for all 387 scan pages, including repository covers, later identifying slips, envelopes, duplicate images and fragments. Some damaged passages remain only partly transcribed. Folders 4–7 described in the finding aid were not supplied as manuscript scans and are outside this edition.

PREPARATION AND REVIEW
Prepared for Volney Douglas with AI assistance in September 2026, from visual reading of the supplied scans. Each scan received an initial reading. Version 1.1 adds local OCR processing of all 193 pages containing the original 1,200 uncertainties, and applies selected image-checked corrections. OCR completion is not a complete new visual check; the item ledger records which passages were individually adjudicated. The page inventory identifies the review level. This edition has not received an independent human transcription check or repository approval. It is offered for scholarly review and correction, and does not replace the source images.

INDEPENDENCE
The first edition was prepared separately from the plain-English family edition. For version 1.1, the other agent’s contextual review and the family edition were consulted to guide image checks; this revision is not a blinded comparison. Part of a pre-existing 25-page transcription was viewed during the initial inventory, before the request for independence; it has not been used for this transcription. The work is therefore not a blind experiment.

WORDING AND LAYOUT
Original wording, spelling, grammar, abbreviations and recoverable punctuation are retained. No silent expansion or modernization is intended. Handwritten capitalization, punctuation and word division sometimes require judgment. Superscripts are lowered to the baseline. Ordinary line wrapping is reflowed; verse lines, addresses and separate text blocks are distinguished. Underlining and decorative features are not systematically encoded. This is a direct reading transcript, not a facsimile or a fully diplomatic edition.

UNCERTAINTY NOTATION
Double square brackets contain editorial intervention. [[illegible]] means writing could not be read; an unspecified gap does not imply a word count. Adjacent unreadable spans may be combined. A stated extent, such as [[illegible: approximately 3 words]], is an estimate. [[word?]] gives a tentative reading, including tentative phrases. [[lost: torn edge]] records writing missing at a damaged edge; [[obscured: fold]] identifies an obstruction. Original single brackets remain as written. No missing wording is supplied solely to produce a fluent sentence.

CORRECTIONS AND SEPARATE BLOCKS
[[deleted: text]] records readable canceled text; [[deleted: illegible]] records an unread cancellation. [[inserted: text]] records a significant insertion in its intended position. [[margin]] and [[/margin]] enclose marginal writing. [[postmark]] and [[postage]] identify postal text. [[blank]], [[seal]] and [[sketch]] identify useful non-textual features. A [[separate block: description]] marker introduces an independently positioned passage. Handwriting or cancellation may itself be uncertain.

SOURCE ORDER AND LATER DESCRIPTIONS
Entries follow the PDFs’ scan order. Where the leaves appear out of sequence, the editorial note suggests a reading order without moving the entry. Later identifying slips are transcribed separately; their names, dates and attributions are not silently substituted for manuscript readings. Duplicate scans retain separate entries. Apparent contradictions are recorded rather than reconciled.

DOCUMENTARY STATEMENTS
The letters preserve their writers’ assertions, beliefs, rumors and opinions. Transcription does not establish those statements as historical fact. Historical racial language and other offensive wording remain where legible. The original scans are the evidence against which this typescript should be checked.

REFERENCES AND CORRECTIONS
A page ID such as 05-025 means source 05, PDF scan page 25, counting the cover as page 1. A block ID such as 05-025-P02 adds the second transcript paragraph or block. These are editorial locators; they are not manuscript page numbers. Suggested citation: George Douglas Papers annotated transcription, version 1.1, 05-025-P02; corresponding source 05, scan page 25. For a correction, give that locator, the existing wording, a proposed reading, and the image or other evidence supporting it.

FILES FOR REUSE
The package includes four searchable PDFs and four editable Word files, a complete UTF-8 transcript, structured JSON, an offline HTML reading copy, a page inventory, and an unresolved-readings register in TSV and HTML. The register retains the original uncertainty IDs; resolved IDs are retired and new IDs use higher numbers. The review ledger preserves all 1,200 baseline items. The register includes tentative readings, unread text, loss and obstruction; structural markers and readable insertions or deletions are not unresolved items. Original source PDFs are identified by filename and SHA-256 checksum but are not duplicated in this package.

REPOSITORY CREDIT
Original documents and scans: George Douglas Papers, 1830–1939, Center for Restoration Studies MS 5, Abilene Christian University Special Collections and Archives, Brown Library, Abilene Christian University, Abilene, Texas. Collection address printed in the supplied files: https://digitalcommons.acu.edu/george_douglas. This contribution acknowledges the repository’s work in preserving and scanning the collection. No repository endorsement or transfer of copyright is asserted.

TRANSCRIPTION GUIDANCE
The general approach of retaining original spelling and abbreviations and marking uncertainty is informed by the Smithsonian Transcription Center’s How to Transcribe guidance, consulted September 2026: https://transcription.si.edu/instructions/transcribe. This edition defines its own notation and is not a Smithsonian project.

About this collection
Title: George Douglas Papers

01-003-P02
Creator: George Douglas

01-003-P03
Identifier/Call Number: Center for Restoration Studies Manuscripts #5

01-003-P04
Physical Description: 1 linear feet (2 boxes)

01-003-P05
Dates (Inclusive): 1830-1939

01-003-P06
Dates (Bulk): 1830-1939

01-003-P07
Location: Center for Restoration Studies

01-003-P08
Language of Materials: English

01-003-P09
Scope and Content Note: Photocopies of documents belonging to George Douglas, including a Civil War journal describing the battle of Sabine Pass, Texas, a land grant certificate from the governor of Texas, and two photos of Douglas. Includes photocopies of the day book of Nathaniel Lynn Douglas, beginning in 1830, loan and legal papers for the Douglas family, and correspondence between Douglas family members.

01-003-P10
Biographical Note: George Douglas served in the Confederate Army during the Civil War.

02-003 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 02 - L. Douglas Sermon Notes, 16 Feb 1883.pdf | PDF scan page 3

02-003-P01
Of all the thoughts enclosed in the language of Inspiration [[lost: left edge]] its laws its precepts its promises what is there that conveys more, to our minds and hearts than is expressed in this short sentence? Jesus wept. Let us look a moment at the character of Jesus: who, and what is he? If we accept the truths of the Bible, he is the son of God, The King of the Jews, The bright and morning star! A ruler of the Jews came to Jesus and said, "we know thou art a teacher come from God for no man can do the miracles that thou doest, except God be with him." Look then at the power of this man: by his word water is turned into wine. The blind receive their sight, the lame walk. By His word the tempest is stilled, and He walks the waves of the wild Galilee. By His word the dead are called back to life again. Truly He was the son of God.

02-003-P02
But it is not from the Bible alone that we have testimony in regard to the greatness of this man. Renan in his Life of Christ says "All ages will proclaim that among the sons of men there were none born greater than Jesus." His goodness, [[greatness?]], his omnipotent power, we cannot doubt. We stand in awe as we contemplate them, and our hearts acknowledge him as King of Kings and Lord of Lords. But while we contemplate all of this glorious majesty our eye falls upon this simple sentence Jesus wept. What do we learn by this? by these two short words. We learn that He had a heart that could be touched by the sorrows and Suffering of our frail humanity: that Sympath[[lost: torn edge]]

EDITORIAL NOTE: First word and left edge of the second line are damaged. The terminal word is torn; it has not been completed from context. Text continues on scan page 4.

====================================================================
02-004 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 02 - L. Douglas Sermon Notes, 16 Feb 1883.pdf | PDF scan page 4

02-004-P01
the power to "make e'en the wretched blessed" had its abode in His breast and that He could be touched by a feeling of our infirmities. How our hearts thrill with emotions of Love and thankfulness when we think of this blessed truth, that He was in sympathy with the sorrows of frail humanity.

02-004-P02
More than eighteen hundred years have been measured by the revolving Earth since Jesus walked the shores and waves of Galilee, but the influence of His words and life, are still felt. And down through the vista of the coming Ages they will be felt till time shall be no more. And with all the records of His wonderful life this simple truth will descend with the others, Jesus wept.

02-004-P03
L. Douglas.
Sylvana Feb 16th 1883

02-004-P04
Forever reign victorious King
Wide through the earth thy name be known;
And call our longing Souls to sing,
Sublimest anthems near thy throne.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continues the damaged sentence at the end of scan page 3. The final four lines are verse.

03-003 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 03 - Sylvana Church Endorsement for George Douglas, Sept 1888.pdf | PDF scan page 3

03-003-P01
This certifies that Bro. George Douglas, who is a member of the Church of Christ worshiping at Sylvana Ellis County Texas, has for some length of time been laboring in word and doctrine, and wishing him to continue in the work, we take pleasure in stating that he goes with our full permission & hearty approval. May he realize with the Apostle Paul. "Woe is me if I preach not the Gospel of Christ" - Ever remembering the Solemn Charge to Timothy to preach the Word &c -

EDITORIAL NOTE: Several letters fade at the right edge. Hyphenation in Gospel at the line break is joined. The final abbreviation is read as &c.

I was detailed to go down on Red River & join the Command at [[Walkers?]] Bend - but it has not passed I expect them Every [[illegible]] The Sabine laying up - I cant leave - Our field & [[illegible]] yet.

All at home to Eliza & family - also [[illegible]] well on [[illegible]] furlough I suppose you have heard of the death of Uncle Alfred he died on the 5th of June from a wound Recd on the 18th of May - The Gentleman awaits on me & [[illegible]] you a too bad [[illegible]]. Please write to me all the news immediately Let me know how you are all getting along I have been going to [[illegible]] - Wish I could come & see you but I could [[illegible]].

04-004 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 4

04-004-P01
Brushy Ellis County Texas
Friday Jan 29th 1864

04-004-P02
Mr George Douglas
Dear [[Bro?]] & Cousin,

04-004-P03
By the above Caption You are informed of my Whereabouts After an absence of twenty odd months I arrived at [[illegible]] on 22d of the month Since which time I have mingled with the friends of the [[illegible]]. Truly I have had a pleasant time - The courteous manner in which I have been every where received and the kindness shown to me has been truly gratifying to me personally. I have just returned from Aunt Betties and concluded I would chat you a little while through the medium as a substitute, to an oral one.

04-004-P04
I wrote to you last Sept but have never learned the destination of my letter - [[deleted: illegible]] you did not return it, [[deleted: illegible]] if you did I did not reply [[illegible]] this one will not prove offensive to you. Since Feb 1st - I have been on detached Service the greater portion of the time - Last Feb was sent to the Ouachita river Country to gather forage for the Command. Was engaged in this business two months. [[illegible]] of my [[Solitariness?]] passed more pleasantly the first month excepted than [[illegible]] to you all "bibs", and as a matter of course, [[illegible]] pleased more than the ladies in the prettiest Looks - Smiles & kindest [[illegible]] and when the pot boiled the strongest and being not entirely [[illegible]] of the influence of the gentle fair, on some occasions I [[illegible]] their claims. And was almost persuaded (I like to have said taken unawares) [[illegible]] the "equal" in [[gentility?]] & [[illegible]] of the Young ladies of my former [[illegible]] - The brindle of my acquaintances [[illegible]] shirts [[illegible]] as they seem to have especial claims of Superiority [[illegible]].

04-004-P05
But Enough on this subject as it is hardly entitled to a place in these times of strife & Confusion - Whilst the all absorbing all engrossing theme is War War - its stern realities & consequences Which at present our Country has begun to feel the Weight of considerably.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Friday Jan 29th 1864 is the reading of the heading. Extensive dark photocopy interference obscures words; the social comparison in the lower half particularly needs review. The text continues in the upper upright block of scan page 5.

into the Service, Where it Seems we all arrive at the Same, now in October Col Burleson having become famous as a “Rosin Heel” catcher was Sent to this State on the Same business, Where we were mortified [[illegible]] Texas Contained quite a [[illegible]] of the above Character. I am very anxious to know how They came off at the Chattanooga fight. 04-010 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 10

04-010-P01
Camp [[Yavely?]] Texas
March 13/65 —

04-010-P02
Cousin George

04-010-P03
Dear Sir

04-010-P04
I acknowledge your Kind Letter of the 1st inst which inspired many pleasant emotions especially the part Relating to our association in years gone by — But while enjoying those things one is Drawn in Retrospection and reflecting upon the return of a Long absent Brother all joy has been turned into sadness by the Intelligence of the Death of your Sister. All had been fill with sanguine emotions of gladness and joyous are the recollections of by gone times. Cousin George In this dark hour of bereavement you have my Sympathies. My tongue can give vent to but little yet I sincerely have ever been Sorry Although the fleeting of Life are overtaken by the Cold relentless Clay are moved to an [[illegible]] of mortality thro our own frailness then [[illegible]] our comfort [[illegible]] duty and happiness

EDITORIAL NOTE: Camp name and some wording in the latter part of the paragraph are uncertain in the dark scan. The date reads March 13/65. Continues on scan 11.

We are all well here and Continue to have Agreeable times We Came to this place the 5th of March and will more [[illegible]] very likely be to the Brazos County. Through him [[illegible]] I Recd a Letter from Brother James which I enclose to you.

04-011-P02
Lafayette is desirous of a transfer to this Department. Mat Brawner of this Co desires To be transferred to [[Saffold's?]] Command & has [[illegible]] Capt [[Wad?]] on the subject — The Capt Will make an Effort to have it arranged. Nothing save an immediate peace is more heartily wished by me.

04-011-P03
I am looking for Fayette with Sam'l [[Rea?]] in a few days. I have lately heard from Aunt [[Matten?]] all here was quite well. Write & Address me at Hempstead which is Corps headquarters.

04-012 | verse
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 12

04-012-P01
The River of death

04-012-P02
Beautiful world! I've trusted long
To your gilded show, your Syren Song;
I thought the joys you gave would last
But one by one they have hurried past;
Till now the last you could give has flown,
And I stand by the River, Alone! Alone!

04-012-P03
I was merry once, and to music sweet
Kept tinkling time with dancing feet;
I crowned my hair with roses red,
But the lute is shatter'd, the roses dead;
Music and blossoms are long since gone,
And I stand by the River, Alone! Alone!

04-012-P04
And once I loved; 'twas a dream of bliss,
Could earth give more to her child than this?
Rapture so great it was almost pain,
A girdle of flowers with a silken chain;
But love like a vision of night has flown,
And I stand by the River, Alone! Alone!

EDITORIAL NOTE: Manuscript copy of a poem. The title and verse lineation are retained. Continues on scan 13.

====================================================================
04-013 | verse and letter
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 13

04-013-P01
Then madly I quaffed the cup of fame,
And smiled when thousands lisp'd my name,
My brow was bound with a wreath of bay,
But it faded quickly and drooped away,
Leaf after leaf until all are gone,
And I stand by the River Alone! Alone!

04-013-P02
And what have I done O Christ! for thee?
Drear the visions my dim eyes see!
Have I given a cup of water cold
To a single Lamb in Thy precious fold?
Time for working is past and gone,
And I stand by the River Alone! Alone!

04-013-P03
O Christ! have mercy! The waters wild
Are sweeping away Thy erring child;
Wilt Thou not listen? O, take my hand,
And guide me through to a distant land;
All that I trusted on earth has flown,
Must I sink in the River, Alone! Alone!

04-013-P04
The River! The River! tis dark and wide,
But I see a light on the other Side!
My heart no longer is faint with fear,
For Heaven has help; my God is near!
With joyous footsteps I hasten on
No more in the River, Alone! Alone!

04-013-P05
[[separate block: accompanying letter]]

04-013-P06
Bloomsdale July 6th/65

04-013-P07

04-013-P08

I think them very beautiful — and this Express doubtless the position of every persons decease.

04-013-P09
Your Cousin
James

04-017 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 17

04-017-P01
[[Yellow?]] Branch [[illegible]]
Sept 18th 1863

04-017-P02
Cousin George. I have written Several times to you but Silence has been the result. My uncertain Whereabouts perhaps have been the Cause — for I cannot think you so ungenerous as not to have Written to me Since Sept last year. I have thought my letters of last Spring may have Contained something not acceptable — if so I cannot conceive what it was — for if you are like the majority of Soldiers — your Feelings could hardly be ruffled — I will try you another Effort and if Silence is the response I may know Something is wrong in Denmark, & will call off my hounds. But as I am floating in rather a dull atmosphere I will not promise to be very interesting but trust I may for a while relieve you of the monotony of the Camps.

04-017-P03
I am home on a detail after Clothing for our Co. having reached Sat Evening. I find things moving on in this vicinity about like they did before the War began save the absence of Young men. Plenty Seems Smiling at every door — and were it not for absent ones I believe the people here would be happy. [[illegible]] quite warm weathering Carried on in North Carolina. As yet I have visited but Little. I have visited Waxahachie twice — a very important case being under Consideration in the Court — To wit; The murder of a Mrs Claunch by her Daughter — The case has not yet been decided but from the evidence I have heard believe she will come clear — but not in the Eyes of the people — as innocent of the offence. From Waxa on Monday I went to Red Oak Camp Ground — Camp meeting being on hand — and have remained till Tuesday — The meeting not so largely attended as in former years — Especially by the Sterner Sex — but there has been no discount on the other Side. The eating part received marked attention on my part. I indulge freely in those Substantials that keep Soul & body together — but did not Labor very hard to make myself agreeable — or to create a Contagious Sometimes Called a “palpitation of the heart” — My observation teaches me That the germs of this malady are in an [[illegible]] State and only need Sunshine to bring them to bloom, Expand & Captivate —

04-017-P04
They had Shouting times that night — Some things done rather disorders — Some Singular movements. Amongst them was a Soldier friend — who yielded to the Solicitation of an Old Gentleman who has a Daughter that has [[illegible]] Claims upon him — I think the meeting is Still in operation — If Shoutings are an indication of Godliness — This is Certainly the most Christian people in the range of my knowledge. Possibly this may be attributable to the fact of the abundance of Preachers — who Claim exemption by reason of their vocation and fearing if they do not all Preach they will have to Shoulder their musket & Battle Some

EDITORIAL NOTE: Letter in a small, closely written hand with a fold and scattered reproduction damage. The first word of the place-name and several clauses remain uncertain. Continues on scan 18.

04-018 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 18

04-018-P01
I will now tell you Something of the operations of our Command. In June it was ordered West & have just returned to about where we! The [[illegible]] was Countermanded & it with another [[illegible]] returned to the vicinity of [[illegible]] Remained in the Same about a month recruiting our horses. About The 1st of Aug. We moved to the Miss. Valley near Natchez — remained there a Couple of Weeks & then were ordered to Our former field (Ark). On the 31st of Aug the Brigade Started on their way rejoicing. We had Seen the Elephant in La & the Boys were willing to go any where. They will doubtless See Some other large Animal in Ark for they will have to ride it as they Left it. [[deleted: illegible]] The Division will be home before there — and they will have a tough time of it with Genl Steele. Majors' Div is also gone to take the [[illegible]] of Walker's [[illegible]] — Had taken up line of march for [[illegible]] & it was believed they would follow also — So now you may listen to hear of Stirring times this fall.

04-018-P02
Whilst Scouting on the Miss Our Squad turned over about 30 fine Horses their arms &c to the Yankee Sent [[illegible]] of them went down to New Orleans to get some Crackers. The others made their Escape Swimming & hiding in the [[weeds?]]. They were Completely Surprised at 3 oclk AM on the 28 of Aug — and a great Wonder all did not get [[killed?]]. Capt [[Vardeman?]] again Got into [[illegible]] Some Candy — He & the dismounted men are now at home [[illegible]] the election — I was not in the Regt yet & Some [[illegible]] of my mess but [[illegible]] had not come out on [[illegible]]. Bob got to Camps the day before I left — I had had chills for a month in the Summer was Sick last Evening & Expect them returning — Jeffie was down last week.

04-018-P03
We Learn Lizzie spent Sunday with you & she Returned to John on Monday. [[Laura?]] Spent last night here. Mother has had a very Sick Spell is getting over it. Had a fine Rain day before yesterday. [[Br?]] Sam Austin Called in today Returning from the meeting — The same is Still going on [[illegible]] the Camp but has been dispersed with only 7 professions So far — We recently Recd a Letter from Lafayette of [[date?]] 1st of Aug. Their Brigade had a heavy fight on the 22nd of July — their Regt went into Battle with 320 & Came out with about 80 — James who was in the [[illegible]] had a narrow Escape [[illegible]] All are Stout & healthy — They are [[illegible]] in high hopes of Early End of the War. I hope their [[illegible]] may be realized.

04-018-P04
[[margin]]
Since [[illegible]] — Those of their Co that was not killed had to Surrender & while They were Stacking arms Hage [[illegible]] through [[illegible]] & Clem [[illegible]] was found at hind [[illegible]] My paper is full — I hope to hear from you before I leave for the army — Yours Truly
H D
[[/margin]]

04-019 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 19

04-019-P01
Cold Springs Texas
Sept 19th /63

04-019-P02
Mr George Douglas

04-019-P03
Dear cousin

04-019-P04
Perhaps you will think strange of receiving a letter from me as you have in all probability forgotten your old hunting companion and school mate it has been A long time since we used to meet down on old Brushy and give the old gobblers a round. and I fear it will be a longer one Still before we can have that blessed privilege again. nearly Four years have pass Since I Saw you and I imagined that you are the Same old george you used to be. I have not changed in the least, only from the big to the bigger. I weigh 175 pounds and the largest Devil in co. (F.) I was ordered to report to the Brigade Quarter [[Mr?]] for fatigue duty this morning. got half way turned round and came back and now looking for the guard to arrest me and if they do I will lye out of it Some how.

04-019-P05
We are having A very pleasant time at this camp duty easy Drill Four evry day except Sundays [[as wet foots?]] we also have a chain guard around our camp wont let a fellow out with his Saddle. you ought to See the boys riding all over the Country bare back There is a big meeting going on in camp. There was about forty mourners last night. They haven't got me yet but I dont know how Soon they will for evry time the old fellow preaches Scare [[there?]] is A [[frog?]] in my throat as big as your fist

EDITORIAL NOTE: Letter continues on scan 20. Original informal spelling is retained. “as wet foots” and the wording before “is A” are difficult and may warrant revision.

04-021 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 21

04-021-P01
Tupelo Mississippi Jan 25th/65

04-021-P02
Dear Brother

04-021-P03
As Brother Lafayette will Start home in a few days on furlough I will write you a few lines to let you know how I am getting along I was painfully wounded on the 22nd of July while Charging the enemys works The ball Struck me on the the left wrist and passed throw my hand my wrist Bones was badly fractured also the Bone of the middle finger I have Suffered a great deal with it about the middle of August I took the fever and for too months and for two months I were hardly able to get off of my bunk with out assistance — I Received 60 days furlough in October to go to mobile to See our relatives — When I arrived at Demopolis my hand was paining me very much and I was very unwell So I Concluded to Stop a few days untill I could get better I Stoped with an old Lady By the name of Brassfield Thair I had all the attention a confederate Soldier Could wish for. By the [[time?]] I was able to go on I had Concluded [[to spend?]] my furlong in that neighborhood & go to Mobile

EDITORIAL NOTE: The repeated “the” and repeated “for … months” are in the manuscript and are retained. The last two lines are crowded and partially obscured. Continues on scan 22.

04-022 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 22

04-022-P01
Some other time. I spent the time very pleasantly & only Regreted that I Couldent Stay longer I Shall never forget the kind people of Ala.

04-022-P02
At the expiration of my furlough I Started to the Command. But learning that I Couldent get to it. I turned my course and went to Columbus Miss. with the intention of getting my furlough extended and go on to Mobile. Before I Could get off from that place the Federals Destroyed the R Road So that I Could not get thair So I went in to the Hospital and remained thair untill a few days ago.

04-022-P03
My hand is not well yet. it has healed up on both Sides untill the Sear is the Size of a pea and it not heal any more it is running all the time The Doctors Say that is Caused by deceased Bone or by Some pieces of Bone that aught to have been removed My wrist will be entirely Stiff I think I will have the use of all of my fingers except the middle one.

04-022-P04
I Returned to the Command last week found the Boys all in good health Thair is very few of our Brigade left only about 4 hundred out of five Regiments

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continuation of scan 21. “deceased Bone” is retained as the writer appears to have written it; no medical correction is substituted.

04-025 | envelope

04-027 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 27

04-027-P01
Monday Morning.

04-027-P02
Dear Zella,

04-027-P03
Of course you would like to know of the meeting. While there has been no further additions, yesterday was a “big” day. According to announcement there was dinner on the ground. And such another dinner. About the finest ever spread at Sylvana. Among visitors present was White Stirman and [[deleted: illegible]] wife, (who sends her love to you) and Nellie Russell. They left after preaching last night for Ennis, White and family going to move right away to Marlin, where White has a job of running a “Watkins” Medicine wagon.

04-027-P04
From Oak Cliff were three carloads of people, Jeff Barclay among the number who kindly remembered you, the wife of J. C. Estes, the preacher, Bro. Stevens, mother wife and sister, Bro. Melton and family and Bro. Holsenback, and wife.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continuation on scan 28. Personal names are transcribed as read; no attempt has been made to identify these visitors from outside records.

====================================================================
04-028 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 28

04-028-P01
These enjoyed themselves finely, and were profuse in their invitations for me to visit them when in Dallas.

04-028-P02
Mrs. Dr. Mitchell and family, and Bro. Dunn's family were out from Waxahachie. These comprise the major part of the visitors who took dinner. After dinner Mrs Howard and family came in and remained until after night preaching. She was showered with attentions by all who knew her while she was with us, and last Cliff Stanford came much to the delight of his many friends.

04-028-P03
The meeting may close tonight, and maybe tomorrow night. Bro. Dunn being something of a revivalist is blue over his work here, he is disappointed.

04-028-P04
No change to report in Lucile's condition but I expect to finish this letter at Red Oak this evening; and will give latest dates.

04-028-P05
Larkin is much worse; the disease taking a malignant form day before yesterday. I will hear from him this morning. All company has been forbidden. The prospect is most serious.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continuation of scan 27.

====================================================================
04-029 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 29

04-029-P01
We will have to hedge on expenses. The Ellis Co. cotton crop is a failure today the eleventh of August. The worms have completely destroyed all the bolls and squares, and blooms are scarcer than I ever saw — if anything is made it has to be made in the future, there is nothing now. Worms are of all sizes and this argues that they will be with us some time yet.

04-029-P02
I hope you will continue to do well, get some weight and strength and hold both. I am feeling fine, batching must agree with me; but I am not stuck on it.

04-029-P03
Am getting from two to eight eggs per day.

04-029-P04
At Kate's. I found Lucile not so well as last Sunday — She seems quite unwell, suffering considerably. Kate says: “Her stomach has gone back on her.” Poor Child, she looks badly. Larkin is about the same as [[illegible]] (Saturday) If he has strength to stand it he may pull through all right.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continuation of scan 28. The pencil postscript is much fainter than the ink text. One word or phrase after “same as” remains unread.

====================================================================
04-030 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 30

04-030-P01
Sermon to the Church to day —
Ila Duncan and Mayfaire made the confession and will be baptized this evening — meeting will close tomorrow night.

04-030-P02
Came up today and had a real civilized dinner with [[Bros?]] Duncan. I dont see why all women can't cook. Its so much nicer —

04-030-P03
Jesse Ligon and family have returned to Red Oak, bag and baggage. I am glad they have come back as Jess is the best Ligon of the whole bunch — Did I tell you that the old wooden center of Red Oak burned out from brick to brick? Well the old rat traps are gone. Some loss heavily —

04-030-P04
This covers the ground to date —
Affectionately G. D.

04-030-P05
Do the best you can for yourself while you are there. Dont bother about me.
My peach preserves were highly praised

04-030-P06
[[margin]]
some of everything is made it has to be [[lost: reproduced line ends]]
[[/margin]]

04-032 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 32

04-032-P01
Wednesday Eve

04-032-P02
Dear Zella, Just got your letter. I staid with Clarence & Kate Sunday night and saw you in the car window Monday morning as you passed.

04-032-P03
Came on home in the evening, and today went back for the laundry and the young lady. Had a puncture coming home and got mighty hot and dirty fixing it. Delivered the young lady at her home and in a few hundred yards returning the same wheel went down again. Had a time. Some men came along and all of us put together could not fix it, so I drove to Rockett on the rim, and with the help of Sid Farrar soon had it righted up. Came on and spent three hours with Kate. The children surely look bad. Glenn was not well, but Kate looks much better than I expected.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Letter continues on scan 33.

====================================================================
04-033 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 33

04-033-P01
They will have to give up that place the first of August. Will likely come to Roland's house in the pasture.

04-033-P02
The letter to you from Paris I opened to see if it should be forwarded. Find it enclosed. Will send yours to your Aunt Nannie by way of explanation.

04-033-P03
I do hope your appetite will stay with you and that the girls will see to your wants. Dont be a burden and dont overdo yourself.

04-033-P04
Dont worry about me. I boiled me two potato's and six eggs yesterday evening, and Ruth gave me a glass of plum jelly and I have plenty of bread, and I reboiled the Molasses and strained it through the new sifter. So I am prepared to get fat. Elmer Carter is very glad to get all of the Cow. Rain plenty since you left. Tell Edwina I enjoyed reading her good letter hugely.

04-033-P05
Affectionately
G. D.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Closing continuation of scan 32. “get all of the Cow” is retained as written; the wording has not been expanded into an inferred meaning.

====================================================================
04-034 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 34

04-034-P01
[[deleted: Monday]] [[inserted: Tuesday]] Morning.

04-034-P02
Dear Zella —

04-034-P03
Yours written Sunday came yesterday. I am glad you did not have a bad time last week, and hope you will do better this week. I know Ed's expenses must be enormous; but be sure you do not add any to them. Our house keepers packed up their goods Saturday, and Sunday Evening loaded them on two wagons, and Monday morning pulled out for Bartlett Texas. At Waxahachie they shipped their effects, and by now are at their destination.

04-034-P04
So my prospects for baching grows brighter from day to day. Sure I live better, and my next cook must look refined. My nieces cant understand why I persist in staying at home, and there is not much use for it except I hate for the house to be unoccupied at night. Elmer Carter has taken the Cow to his house, and there is nothing to do but gather up a very few eggs of an evening.

EDITORIAL NOTE: The weekday was corrected above the original “Monday.” Continuation on scan 35.

====================================================================
04-035 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 35

04-035-P01
The hens are on strike mostly. They mainly water just below the Feterita patch at a swamp spring, and the feterita furnishes them fine picking.

04-035-P02
Our meeting crowds are small, and the preaching not much bigger. It chafes Bro. Dunn very perceptibly, but he has a poor way of helping himself, as the job of building up a large audience is a big one. The crowds will be apt to grow later on.

04-035-P03
Word came down last night that Olaf wished to be baptized, so Bro. Duncan returned immediately to Red Oak to make preparation and Bro. Dunn and others went up after Church was over to attend to it. I have no word from them up to this writing. Dr. Stoker told the friends that apparently he had [[deleted: only]] only a few days to live. This is hard on Kate but not unexpected. Lucile can sit up in a rocker an hour or so each day. I will close this when I hear from them after awhile.

04-035-P04
At Kate's Tuesday Eve. Olaf is resting fairly well now. Looks bad, and very much emaciated. Plenty of friends here, which [[inserted: makes]] them feel that they are not neglected. Do not note any change in Lucile —

04-035-P05
Affectionately G. D.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Closing continuation of scan 34. A repeated canceled word and an insertion in the pencil postscript are recorded.

====================================================================
04-036 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 36

04-036-P01
Thursday Morning

04-036-P02
Dear Zella,

04-036-P03
Olaf died yesterday evening at six thirty. I am just ready to start up there. Was there a couple of hours yesterday morning. They had all the attention from the outside that was needful for their comfort.

04-036-P04
Lucile is still able to sit up a little while every day.

04-036-P05
Larkin is sick with [[deleted: bl]] blood poisoning. Was there a couple of hours yesterday evening. To begin with a hen scratched him on the thumb some days ago, this inflamed and was very painful. That got some better and now he has a large red spot on top of his right foot with red streaks running most up to the knee, he has some fever but is not suffering much. Mrs. Wadley, the Dr's mother died yesterday evening.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Letter continues on scan 37. The partial canceled start of “blood” is recorded.

====================================================================
04-037 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 37

04-037-P01
Red Oak
Thursday Evening — Just back from Olof's burial at the Rawlins cemetery — Many friends and sympathizers were present. Bro Dunn made a good comforting talk at the tabernacle. Twas very hot but I stood it pretty well — I cannot say what effect all of this death and funeral will have on Lucile yet — She seems to have considerable vitality and may linger for quite awhile — Hattie Stanford is to be baptized this evening. Quite a lot of the Patrick Chapel people were down today, and quite a lot are reported coming from Oak Cliff Sunday —

04-037-P02
I hope you are doing finely this week.

04-037-P03
Affectionately G. D.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Closing continuation of scan 36. The name spelled “Olaf” on scan 36 appears as “Olof” here; the difference is retained.

====================================================================
04-038 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 38

04-038-P01
Thursday Morning.

04-038-P02
Dear Zella,

04-038-P03
I am still Baching and doing fine. Somehow I do not expect my housekeepers any way soon.

04-038-P04
Mary Roberts sent down and got all the Fruit jars she wanted some days ago, and yesterday Evening Edna came and got seventeen for her Mother.

04-038-P05
I got some of the peaches from the tree up by the mustang vine, and yesterday I put up a gallon and a quart of preserves all by myself. I had 12 pounds of peaches, and 9 pounds sugar, began at sun up and got through by noon. I did not use a single drop of water. Have disposed of 13 dollars worth of potato's and have plenty left. Kate and children are doing as well as could be expected.

04-038-P06
Nothing new. Affectionately G. D.

EDITORIAL NOTE: The name in the salutation has a flourished initial, read here as Zella, as in adjacent letters. No date other than the weekday is supplied in this entry.

04-046 | typescript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 46

04-046-P01
Field Notes for Bruce Hedrick.

04-046-P02
Being 70.0 acres of the A.J. Porter Survey, Abst. No. 829, in Ellis County, Texas, described as follows:

04-053-P01
I would love for you to be here, for I get very blue and lonesome at times, and you are all I have that is best to live for. With the number of my years I cant stay here long, so your company is that much more precious. I may survive this trouble and may not. At all events my physical strength will be greatly weakened, and for this reason I wanted the car sold, as my car cranking days are over.

Good apples here sell from a dollar to a dollar and a half per bushel. Cider fifty cents to a dollar per gallon. Pears 1.00 to 1.50 per bushel.

04-065 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 65

04-065-P01
Wednesday 11-23-
My Dear Child.
Yours of Monday just to hand and I am glad of the favorable report. Maybe we will have enough to "get by" on. The healing process is doing nicely, the only trouble is I have to keep my jaw still, and this cuts out the Eating act to two meals a day. I told the Dr. I was willing to cut it out to every two or three days if it would help matters. My general health is as good as it ever was.

EDITORIAL NOTE: The heading is transcribed without supplying a month.

====================================================================
04-066 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 66

04-066-P01
2
Now as to the old house.
I have got to tighten up my room for this winter. I noticed last winter that the cold air came in very perceptibly through the ceiling joints. There is three ways to stop this. Ceil over the old ceiling with new lumber, paper or paint the walls.
I put it up to you to fix it, as I may not get home in time to do it before cold weather.
The front of the house needs repainting, and it would be the quickest

====================================================================
04-067 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 67

04-067-P01
3
and perhaps the cheapest and the best to paint.
If this is done the smell would be out by cold weather. The walls would have to have a good coat of oil first to keep the paint from peeling off. Oil, paint, and putting it on, will likely cost about fifty dollars; but you might get it done cheaper. Paper might look nicer; but you could not paper overhead with [[inserted: out]] ceeling under the joints, and that would make it [[labor?]] to prepare

You will have to get Ernest to bring from the lumber [[deleted: yard]] yard enough lumber for the Scaffolding. Show him what you want done, and he will know what to get. All the painting on the front was done before by the long ladder pieced out at the bottom.
Do you think you can do this?

04-069 | manuscript

04-069-P01

I wipe the dishes sometimes, help string the beans, shuck the roasting ears, and draw an occasional bucket of water.
The Dr. wont allow me to do any thing that would make me warm, such as pick up apples and make cider. Help is as scarce here as there, and the Dr. cant get help to pick up his apples and make cider. White folks wont work [[inserted: or dont work]], and niggers are scarce. I do not know when I have seen a white man hunting work.

The Dr. has six patients now and may have another next week. Mrs. Johnson is very feeble. Cancer is killed, but the after effects of Xray are almost as bad. I am hurrying to get this off so you will get it Saturday.
Affectionately Papa.

04-072-P01
Aug 30 - 1923 -
My Dear Child.
I am feeling pretty good this morning. Most of the cancer came out, a little left, cant tell just how much. Dr said a few more treatments he thought would finish up the job.
The lump under my jaw is going away, seemingly getting a little less from treatment to treatment. No indications of trouble there as yet. As to when I can get to come home I cant tell, nor can the Dr tell. That depends on conditions. If no further trouble comes up, and I stay until fully healed, it may be the middle of October

Then go to Waxahachie and go to the tax collector's office, call for my tax receipt, and pay it off by check on Red Oak; then take it Farrar and Kemble attys, and leave with them. They may want

04-075 | manuscript

Have Ernest and Van to make a careful estimate of the cotton crop in Bales. I must know how the crop is turning out and the approximate price.
As to your coming out here, of course I would be glad to have you, and be glad for you to have the trip for a change; but so far there is no need of your coming. I will be very comfortably located with Dr Bird, after Allie leaves, which will be in a few days.
Should you come it will cost at least one hundred dollars for the trip, and if I get along all right there will be [[margin]] no need for it [[/margin]]

====================================================================
04-076 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 76

04-076-P01
5
However you are right in having your arrangements made so as to come at once if needed. Which I most sincerely hope will not be.
Stoker made an awful charge. Did he itemize it? He must have overhauled the whole machine.
Allie is making ready to go to Gunter, take charge of a Dormitory, and put the children in school. At this particular juncture, all things considered, I think a very bad move. She has cancer, and to go away from the Dr. and be gone a year, is taking a risk too great in her case. No telling what a year may bring forth.

====================================================================
04-077 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 77

04-077-P01
6
Plenty of rain here. Corn is green and green mostly in good roasting ear. Cotton is said to be good, also the Boll Weevil.
Days are warm; 80 to 90, nights cool, sleep under cover every night.
Duckworth has been made business manager of the A.W. He has put out a circular letter to the brethren, calling for help to print it in Texas saying he has no money and that the paper has none, and he must have help to get out the first issue. If that dont kill it dead, my guess is off.
Affectionately Papa

04-079 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 79

04-079-P01
Palestine, Sept. 8th 1851
Dear Father,
Your letter of August 16th came to hand a few days ago, and I was much gratified to hear from you all again. I have just been writing to sister Rebecca, and scolded a good deal with [[her?]] for none of you writing to me, but I see from your letter that the fault lay entirely with the mail, so I shall not send it. I am glad to hear of the good health of the Family, and it gives me some hopes that health may be enjoyed in Brushy, as well as other places. This County although reputed to be one of the healthiest in the State, has been very sickly for two months past, more so than I ever saw it in Ellis Co. I think that Old Mr Benson is nearly willing to try the [[illegible]] again for he has not bettered himself so far as health is concerned, by moving down here.
There has been a great many deaths in the county, & five Doctors living in this place have had their hands full of practice, tho' now it is becoming [[margin]] more healthy [[/margin]]

EDITORIAL NOTE: Heavy show-through from the reverse interferes with several readings. The place name following “enjoyed in” reads Brushy after image review.

====================================================================
04-080 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 80

04-080-P01
My own health never was better than it has been since I have been in this place. I still get plenty of work to do. I quit working for Mr Dale about a month ago & have been picking up little jobs round ever since. Were it not so expensive to live here I could make a tolerably fair living. I am now helping build a large Livery Stable, my wages are $1,50 cts pr day, but after subtracting $10 pr month for board & washing, the amount is considerably diminished.
There is going to be a very good opening for carpenters here this coming Winter and Spring, and I am somewhat at a loss whether to continue journeywork or turn out undertaker, I think I could get plenty to do either way.
The Christian Church at this place have resolved to build a house of worship as soon as lumber can be procured. It will be 56 ft long & 34 wide and will present a very neat appearance when finished. It has not rained here for so long that I forget when it did rain last, every thing is very much parched up. Corn crops are tolerably good, but Cotton will not make more than a half crop

EDITORIAL NOTE: Image review reads the verb build before “a large Livery Stable”; the earlier proposed surname has been withdrawn. Show-through is visible throughout.

====================================================================
04-081 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 81

04-081-P01
I was at Mr Mallards house a time ago, he says he is bound for old Rutherford as soon as he can get off, he and his wife have both been very sick, and he says that he cant stand the climate. He is well pleased with the country in other respects [[illegible]] and says if he was to stay in Texas he would move up to your country, but his wife is very much dissatisfied and cant hear to nothing else but going back to Tenn.
I believe I have written all that can interest you. Nothing gratified me more than to hear of Susannahs [[illegible]] the Truth, may she hold out faithful to the end is my sincere prayer. O that all the Family that have arrived at the years of discretion were gathered into the fold of Christ. I long to see you all and it may be that I will visit you between this & Christmas. I send my love to mother brothers & sisters. My respects to the neighbors & friends generally, it is too [[tedious?]] to mention all the names. Write to me one and all of you, and tell me all about every thing. Nothing more at present from
Your affectionate Son.
Henry Douglas

EDITORIAL NOTE: A fold and heavy show-through obscure portions of this page, especially the clause about Susannah.

04-083 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 83

04-083-P01
Jan. 12 1862
Dear Sister Lizzie
I received your letter last week. Beck was here and we had the pleasure of reading it together it had been sometime since we had herd from you. we looked for some of you up Christmas. I had plenty of cake and pies at my cabin Christmas day and a big Egg nog. I envited some of the near neighbors to come and dine with me. and about ten oclock I looked out and saw 14 young men and girls riged out comeing I did not know that they were comeing so you see they stormed me. you may depend the youngsters about here had a lively time of it all the week after which most all the young men left for the war. I wish you and Laura had been here. I have not been any where since christmas week for I have been sick ever since was not well for a week before I am chilling now and have the old [[razzle?]] as heath calls it. [[Gaskin?]] had a chill last week and now has very sore eyes he is walking and is as pretty as any bodys boy he can talk now as good as Angus. Maggie is growing fast and will be another Sister Fleming

EDITORIAL NOTE: Personal names and the colloquial expression before “as heath calls it” should be checked against the original.

====================================================================
04-084 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 84

04-084-P01
her health is good and she is quite hardy but she is lean not as heavy as my man Angus is as fat as a pig and is as full of fight and mischief as he can be Beck has not been very well, she stayed all week with me went home on friday. Warren had gone to deer creek to see about moveing her there as he is going to the war as soon as she is confined they will not break up housekeeping until after that, she is expecting about the 5 to the 11 of Feb. I am wrighting this part to Mother not you as I know she would like to know Warren tryed to get some of her sisters to come home with him and stay a while with her but they could not leave home from some cause or other I dont know of any body she will get to stay with her I told her I would come and stay if she could get no one else we are still in Sams cabin I expect I will remain here some time yet as there is no sign of any thing being done to the other Tell Sue I am glad to hear of her hopes being realized that is her house comenced I wish mine was on the way, but if I can get to be a neighbour to her, I dont care so much if I do have to stay in Sams cabin but when beck goes away I dont know what

04-085 | manuscript

04-085-P01
I will do up on the [[illegible]] and no body very near unless some body comes and lives in their house. Well I believe I have told you about all tell Laura the last time I saw Amanda Berry she asked about her and said she must write to her Lizzie West was here not long ago she says write to her I believe they both claim a letter from her. and she must be sure and write to me is sue ever coming or any of you I recon you will all come now to see Warren before he goes off he will leave as soon as beck is able for him to move her to her fathers. [[you must as come?]] and all write when you can My love to Father and Mother also Sue and Boys
as ever your affectionate Sister
Ellen Douglas

EDITORIAL NOTE: The opening geographical phrase and wording before “and all write” remain unresolved.

04-088 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 88

04-088-P01
Red Oak Tex Sept 6 1892
Dear Sister Lizzie. The reason why I have delayed so long in answering your letter is not indifference or want of affection for a dear absent sister, but rather the dazing effects of some of the contents of yours. I thought silence much easier & more prudent than to say things that, to say the least, might do no good. I do not now wish to say anything only to regret, nay lament such a condition of irreligion as portrayed in your letter Surely California & Sodom & Gomorrah are not unlike
A day in the courts of the Lord is surely worth a thousand in such a place & I surely would make haste to get out, were I there, lest I too might forget God. "Be ye also

04-089 | manuscript

ready for the Son of man cometh in an hour when ye think not" is an injunction not to be forgotten, (the wicked [[deleted: God]] may forget) & I know of nothing so calculated to make us forget as the love of money or the worship of Mammon We have it here. It causes Christians to absent themselves from the house of the Lord, it weakens faith; it dulls the conscience & makes us slothful in that patient continuance in well doing by which we are to inherit the promises. We have just had a good meeting at Liberty conducted by Bros Cole & Graves
You will rejoice with me that Clarence & Kate obeyed the gospel. They will be 14 the 8th inst & I trust when the Lord comes to make up his jewels they will be of

04-093 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 93

04-093-P01
Palestine Dec. 3d 1853
Dear Mother
Time passes rapidly away, & tonight I find myself writing to you instead of being on my road home, as I had wished I often think that it is not worth while to make anything like definite calculations for the future; as I fail, in them so often. The time of my going home seems to be so uncertain that I shall make no promises about it this time. The principal reason of my delay is, that I have, incurred more expences than I anticipated, & shall have to work longer in consequence. I hoped to see Warren, down here last week, but was disappointed I had however the pleasure of meeting with David Gaslin, at Bonners ferry on Trinity, last monday evening & also on Tuesday morning. I learned from him some of the particulars about home, & amongst the rest, that Jepse & Warren had gone out on

EDITORIAL NOTE: The date is read as “3d,” not the “30” on the preceding identifying slip. The given name near the end appears “Jepse” and is retained as read.

04-094 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 94

04-094-P01
Brazos. I am very much behind the times, with regard to your plans & prospects up at home; none of you seem to write on that subject, to any definite extent. True, Warren has been writing a little about his trip to the Brazos, but only casually alluded to his second trip in his last letter; & I suppose that had I gone home now I should have had the pleasure of not seeing him or Jepse either
Now when I do come, I would like to find them both at home; so you must write immediately & let me know when they are coming back
I believe this is all in the way of directions &c, at present, so I must try & think of some news to write Our meeting closed last monday night & was I think quite profitable & interesting. Owing to the cold, bad weather the representation of the neighboring Churches was not so full as we expected The Co-operation will, I think be quite effective; about 450 dols were subscribed for evangelizing purposes, & I

====================================================================
04-095 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 95

04-095-P01
think, with a little exertion, the amount can be doubled. A committee of 5 managers, was appointed, to employ an evangelist & conduct other business of the co-operation; & we hope that great good will be accomplished, the ensuing year
Although several preachers were confidently expected to attend the meeting, yet none of them came, & Bro. Kendrick had all the preaching to do. There were [[6?]] baptized & one other added to the Church 2 of them were little boys, one, Addison, son of Bro Clark aged about 12 years the other Albert, Bro. K's oldest son about 10 years old. They seemed fully to understand (& I have no doubt did) what they were doing. It was a beautiful & interesting scene to see children, young & comparatively innocent, pressing into the Kingdom, & thus consecrating their lives to the Redeemer Whilst others, old & gray, could sit unmoved, under the appeals of the Gospel, nor be persuaded to devote the little remainder of their 3 score & ten, to the only Cause worth living for, & the only one that promises aught for the future

EDITORIAL NOTE: The baptism count is blotted. Fragments of another sheet visible behind the right edge are not a continuous readable passage.

05-003 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 3

05-003-P01
Palestine Tex. Jan. 12 1855
Dear Father:
I have been looking for a letter from home; for a long time, not having heard from any of you since I left there in Oct. But as I have not heard I [[illegible]] to write again [[illegible]] I think it rather uncertain [[illegible]] you would hear from me
Lest you might have failed to get any of my late letters, I would say that I have written several times since November, & this must excuse me if such be the case
I much desired a letter from you before making arrangements for the present year, & I waited until New Years day for that purpose. Being then left entirely on my own judgement I concluded to remain here one year longer, & risk the consequences. Dr Kendrick & myself have formed a partnership for the year. I get ⅓ of the accounts, & am at ⅓ the expence, pay my board & furnish my own horse
The advantages of this arrangement are, — his experience — a share in a practice which is firmly established — and a division of responsibility in bad cases, which is not the least of things to be considered. The disadvantages are,

EDITORIAL NOTE: Abrasion or fading at the folds interrupts the opening paragraph. The shares are read as one-third; the second is faint.

====================================================================
05-004 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 4

05-004-P01
— a probability of making more money elsewhere — the greater incentive to study, by being thrown on my own resources — & it may be the loss of a good field of operations by being 1 year later. — Have I done wisely, or not?
We have great opposition here both from [[numbers?]] & prej[[illegible]] This latter is, however giving [[illegible]] in the minds of the people; & as for the Doctors, it makes but little difference what they think, say, or do. Dr K's practice has been regularly on the increase, for some time, & will continue to do so. Old Mr Ransom, (whom I take to be a man of strong prejudices) has declared his intention to try Dr K when next he wants a physician. Others, the same. I mention him, as he is known to you
I wrote to Warren, of a Cancer case we have on hand, which was considered doubtful, & is probably so yet, but it is now doing well & we have more hope. It was a great risk, but will be a great triumph, if a cure is effected. — My health is good. I think I can venture to promise you a visit in the Spring, say April. I could come sooner, but the winter winds & sleets are too bad. If you have not written please do so immediately. My love to all
Yours in all affection Henry Douglas
N. L. Douglas

EDITORIAL NOTE: Two faded phrases near the top remain unresolved. Medical claims are transcribed as the writer’s historical statements.

05-006 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 6

05-006-P01
Palestine Tex. Ap. 8, 1853
Dear Mother:
The letter from yourself & Susannah, came by last mail, & lest you be somewhat disappointed by my delay in my promised visit, I now drop you a line
I recd a letter from Uncle Sam a short time ago, suggesting the 1st of May, as the most suitable time for me to come up, in consequence of his engagements, at court in April & also Jepie's & Warrens absences, he, thinking it probable that they would not be at home until about that time. As it was of no material difference to me, I wrote him immediately that I would be up the last week in April. & lest the letter should miscarry, I write this. Your letter was nearly 3 weeks on the way & if this makes no better speed, & nothing should hinder, I will be with you soon after you get it. I am pleased that Susannah has consented to come down with me, & hope there will be no disappointment, in any way; but that the step shall prove entirely satisfactory to all concerned. I think you are disposed to praise my humble self entirely too highly in this matter; for I have only done, what seemed to me, a reasonable duty. I feel that I have been blessed beyond my expectations, in whatsoever

====================================================================
05-007 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 7

05-007-P01
I have laid my hand to, since I left your roof to seek my fortune in the wide world. I know that while memory lasts I shall never forget the day when I parted from Father, in the wide lonely prairie, near the Willow pond, his parting advice, & the loneliness of the subsequent hours in walking to Porters Bluff. Never till then had I felt so isolated; never had I felt what it was to be homeless, houseless, & in effect, friendless, for I was going amongst strangers, & I knew not whither I took everything upon trust; & have not been disappointed. I have been blest with health & plenty; not so abundantly as some, but as much as I had any right to hope for. I repeat then, that I deserve no praise, but if I can render any service to others I am only doing my duty.
You may, then, expect me up about the last of April, (of course I can't say precisely) & I hope to remain with you a week or 10 days, maybe longer. The weather has been very dry with us, for a long time, & crops are suffering greatly for rain. Corn has been badly frostbitten & much of it will be to plant again. I fear the wheat has been ruined, with you. I remain as ever, your affectionate Son,
Henry Douglas.
Mrs Mary Douglas

05-007-P02
[[margin]] P.S. Give my love to all. Remember me to [[Un.?]] & aunt Cynthia. &c &c
H.D. [[/margin]]

EDITORIAL NOTE: The postscript is inverted at the top of the image. The abbreviation before “& aunt Cynthia” is uncertain.

====================================================================
05-009 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 9

05-009-P01
Missisipi County, Missouri
June 23d 1860
Dear Father:
After a long lapse of time I now find an opportunity to write you. I have nothing encouraging to write. I have had to pay from 1 to 2 dollars for sheep & have been at a great deal of trouble to find them & expense in collecting my drove. Mr Willis Helm sent $500 by me to get him some sheep & with the 1500 I have bought 750 head & do not know that I will have money enough to get them to Tex. as I only have some 200 left. I bought common sheep in Callaway & Graves Co's Ky purchase lambs at 1 & Ewes at 1.25 per head but could not get enough & so went back to Christian Co & got some 250 head at 1.75 & 2 per head These are finer sheep & I would now prefer that all my funds were invested in such sheep
I crossed the Mississippi at the Iron Banks last Sunday & had to pay 8 cts per head ferriage then after driving two days I had to stop on account of the sheep taking a disease from poison or some thing else. Several have died & altogether I have lost near 50 head since I started, mostly however by their dodging in the thickets the first few days

====================================================================
05-010 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 10

05-010-P01
driving. I learn that every drove of sheep that has passed through here has suffered severely in the same way that mine has. Last year about this time a Mr Long passed through with 1000 & lost 150 to 200. A Mr South, Methodist preacher, with 3 or 400 lost only a few. He passed through about the last of July. A few days ago a drove of 7 or 800 passed from Rutherford Co Tennessee & lost a great many. The people here say that I have escaped better than any of them & they say, further, that after leaving here all the droves did well which I greatly hope is true. I now have my sheep in a large crabgrass pasture free of charge, & will remain till I think it prudent to start which I hope will be in 3 or 4 days. Nearly all are doing finely but probably 50 show a diseased condition. The symptoms are, first a drooping & swelling of the ears, which thicken up the cellular tissue being filled with yellow serum. The sheep becomes dull & stupid & in some cases the head swells — under the jaw the skin will hang in a pouch & I have stuck my knife into several & the yellow serum would flow freely. I know that I have lost 5 since I crossed the river & one more will die, probably. I forget to say that, after the head swells the sheep suffers from an intolerable itching, judging from its actions, & the hair will come off when the swelling goes. I have 2 toll gates

====================================================================
05-011 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 11

05-011-P01
to pass before I get clear of the swamps & then no more I suppose till I get to Fort Gibson ferry on the Arkansas. I have 4 hands hired at 15 dollars per month each & taking all together I cannot make the drove profitable if I do not lose by it. I have not heard from any of you since I left Texas nor have I heard from Ellen since I left Lincoln County. Taking everything into consideration I cannot feel otherwise than "low down" but win or lose I am bound to go through. My health is still good & if I dont get sick I think I can stand it. Now I have written this I do not know that it would be best to send it, but you had better know the truth than to be in suspense. I suppose I will reach Springfield Mo. in 30 or 40 days & you must write me at that place & also at Fort Gibson, & write to Warren to do the same as I am very anxious to hear from all of you. I have been much disappointed & perplexed by men who engaged with me for the trip, failing to come up at the appointed time & some not coming at all. This required me to hire hands temporarily & higher prices & to pay them off when they quit, which has been a heavy draw on my pocket. I will write again — for the present this is enough. Love to all.
Your affectionate son. H. Douglas

05-014 | manuscript

05-014-P01
Murfreesboro Ten
April 18 /54.
My Dear Sir
It has been about two years since I heard from you in relation to our business — it has been I believe nearly three years. I should be glad to have a letter from you — giving me full accounts of every thing, and I hope you will write to me immediately on the receipt of this.
I have bought lately of Gen. A. Sublett the balance of the Littleton White tract of Land — the purchase embraces about thirty two hundred & fifty acres — You know where it is situated & I want you to see to it. See whether the Taxes are paid, and give me any information you may be able to obtain in relation to it.
I would be obliged to you to write me fully about the Louis Dickson tract of Land on the Trinity River (I believe it is called the Louis Dickson tract) — Gen. A. Sublett owns it & I am thinking about buying it. I believe the Land is at the mouth of the five mile [[deleted: branch?]] [[inserted: creek]]
Write me what the Land is worth & give me any information you may have, or can get, in relation to it.

05-027 | typed verse
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 27

05-027-P01
There is a lonliness of heart
No words can ere express,
Too deep for sorrow's tears to start
To relieve the aching breast.
A sunrless, motionless, waveless
waste,
All dark and drearily forelorn
And every lovely flower that
placed upon it, hides a poisonous
thorn.

05-027-P02
1867 G. Douglas

EDITORIAL NOTE: This is a typescript, not handwriting. “lonliness,” “sunrless,” and “forelorn” are retained as printed; lines follow the typescript.

05-036-P01
Bardsdale, Sat, 29th, 1893.

05-036-P02
My Dear Aunt:—I finished up my morning work pretty well this forenoon, so now I will take time to write to you. I am still better—haven’t had a “break down” since I wrote last.

05-036-P03
I have every room swept nicely—I still have a bed in my sitting-room two rockers, two footstools, nicely covered, the stand-table, Mr. [[Curren?]] made, two tea-baskets on the mantle-piece, my vases, & two advertisement pictures, which makes the room presentable.

05-036-P04
I try to keep the door opening in to the hall closed, so as to have the room ready to receive anyone; for they come, at any and all hours, being as Bruce

05-036-P05
[[margin]] This afternoon I made 4 glasses of preserves out of the last quart of my black berries—I haven’t much fruit left. Wont I have a siege filling my jars by and By. [[/margin]]

L. which had never entered my mind & which would be profitably impressed on the mind of every mother, & that is that we are “teaching our children what is in our own hearts whether we try or not”. I paused, in my busy life, & asked what am I teaching mine? I have given three mothers the benefit of the thought. I am surprised that [[Curren?]] has another [[boy?]]. 05-047 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 47

05-047-P01
Bardsdale August 8th. Dearest Aunt:—I have just gotten dinner over. Mr. Headrick has gone to work, Elmo is seated on the table, and Bruce by it with a slate, and talking more. I am as well as usual. Mr. H. helped me wash yesterday. Grandma Humphrey’s growth increases, and is so bad she has difficulty in breathing—consequently has consented to have an operation performed; which, I greatly fear she will not undergo. Dr. [[Hinckley?]] is back now and he Drs. Bard and Mott, are to do the work. I saw Dr. H. passing, once since his return. I spoke, told him I was glad to see him back, and expressed lasting gratitude for his kindness. Could hardly keep from saying if he thought half as much of me, as I did of him, he would have called to see us sooner. but of course we can’t express ourselves thus freely, to strangers.

05-047-P02
We had our picnic at Mr. Grimes, last saturday, which was quite a success. I prepared a good dinner in case I should get a chance to go; but the hour came for starting, and no opportunity presented itself. How fully I realized that if I was back there, or even you here it would be different. Had I acted on my own feelings, I would have put forth no effort to go; but the children were so anxious to go, they said, if I didn’t go down and ask Mr. Wigmore for his cart, they would. I knew they couldn’t bring it alone, so I went with them but fortunately for my “brazen face” it had been spoken for, and I found it out before I had to ask. I found Mrs. Pickering hardly able to be out of bed, and still fixing to go off camping, in hopes ’twould enable her to stay a little while longer with her children. So I swept her house, cooked her two jelly cakes, and came on home about noon. Mr. H. took his dinner so the children and I sat down and ate picnic dinner alone. After we had finished, and I had sat down, to read a little, down came Mr. Henry Chadsey in Mrs. [[Curren’s?]] surrey, for us. Lillie Chadsey had inquired of several why I wasn’t there, and

EDITORIAL NOTE: No year is supplied in this page’s heading. Several personal names have uncertain letter forms.

====================================================================
05-048 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 48

05-048-P01
the answer of all was of course, “I guess she didn’t have any way”. She told them there was no reason I shouldn’t have a way, when there were so many conveyances there, so she started Henry in a hurry. I was thankful on account of the children especially. They had a large table heavily loaded, with choicest food. Mrs. Grimes took up nearly all her dishes, and every one else took plenty so it was a set table just like you would have at your own home. But just such a mixture of dishes, you never heard of. It took them quite awhile to get them straightened out. They had lemonade and ice-cream. two swings a set of croquet, and a game of baseball. And [[no part?]] of the pleasure was due wholly to Lillie Chadsey. I was amused to observe, as she came down in the afternoon, to see Elmo running her. Imagine him handling (or rather attempting to) over Two hundred pounds. But you know he always rather liked her. That was the 4th, and little [[Beckie’s?]] birthday, and what think you, she went to Santa Paula with Mr. [[Reck?]] [[Lehock?]] and married him unbeknowing to her father. The old gentleman is quite wrathy over it. ordered her off the place, when she went back for her clothes, and threatened to disinherit her. It was her 18th birthday. The [[Wilmot?]] [[Hagarts?]] are not all dead yet. Grandma Roberts spent last sunday night with us. I am always so glad to see her. I forgot to say, I heartily wished for you to dine with us, when we sat down to eat our picnic dinner all alone. Elmo said to me one day “when a girl marries a young man, she takes his name, doesn’t she”, to which I said yes, and he continued how many Headricks will there be then? Bruce was standing by, and put in, I’m going to marry a “dutch girl”. Well I must tell you that I earned $4.50 pitting fruit. A handsome amount for me.

05-048-P02
Expecting an account of the meeting soon, I am as ever,
S. G. H.

EDITORIAL NOTE: The phrase before “of the pleasure” appears to read “no part”; it is marked as tentative. Personal names remain uncertain. The earnings are for pitting fruit.

05-064 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 64

05-064-P01
There in mist my vision failing
As bending o’er the iron railing
Vain regrets my soul assailing
Passed in review a life that little fruitage bore.
All its longing and its yearning
All its fruits to ashes turning
All its fever fiercely burning
Through my frenzied brain, the stormy passions tore

05-064-P02
The years to come all desolated
To the thorn and rockwaste fated
Dull despair to sorrow mated
Spread their pall upon the soul and bid hope [[flee?]] door
Clouds above—the darkness falling
Night’s deep’ning gloom in mist appalling
Future years to conflict calling
Conflict with that Fate which life’s disasters bore

05-064-P03
Yesterday brought its roseate vision
Today Fate holds us in derision
Tomorrow in the fields elysian
We’ll dream again the dreams dreamed on life’s
Sunrise shore

EDITORIAL NOTE: Lineation follows the manuscript verse. Image review supports door in the second stanza; the preceding marked word remains tentative.

05-065 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 65

05-065-P01
Now that life is slowly sinking
Light and shadow closely linking
And the soul Nepenthe drinking
Will resign its burdens on the dark Plutonian [[inserted: shore]]
When death’s shiv’ring chill comes o’er us
Which our loved ones felt before us
We may join the blissful Chorus
Which swells the everlasting anthem
Where saints their homage pour.
