05-066 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 66

05-066-P01
In answer to the Author of “Georgie.”

05-066-P02
I seek not for devoted dears.—
“Platonic friendship at my years,”
Most surely will suffice,
Impassioned hours have long gone by—
The furtive glance—the pensive sigh—
[[Was as for lovers nice?]].
Give me a strong and helping hand
While adverse clouds hang low,
A friend in danger’s hour, who’ll stand
Then onward will I go
I’ll meet cold fate, with heart elate
And smile at fortune’s frown,
I’ll bare my breast, to its rudest blast,
Altho’ I may go down,

05-066-P03
“Beauties newblown” will Nature bring
With each returning vernal Spring
And I enjoy these,
The flowrets on the prairies wide
The murmuring streams which sweetly glide
The grand and lofty trees,
Altho’ I am an Anchorite
Absolved from humankind
Some joys will stop with me o’er night
And leave a pledge behind,
That in after years, thro’ changeful cares
They’ll call around once more
And bring with them, some nameless gem
“From the distant Aiden shore,

05-066-P04
Thus Hope is still the harbinger
Of many a blissful coming year
Which fortune may deny.

EDITORIAL NOTE: The sixth line remains a tentative reading. No substitute has been imported from another version of the poem. The closing quotation after “shore” is not visible.

====================================================================
05-067 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 67

05-067-P01
But still I’ll seize the brighter side,
The cheerful hours where pleasures glide
Beneath a sunlit sky,
Today, I’ll take the sweets which come
Tomorrow, then, likewise,
And thus I will be sure of some
From Fortune, as it flies
But idle thought, with love o’erfraught
I’m sure is a burlesque
When sent to one, “whose work is done,”
And’s nothing now to risk.

05-078 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 78

05-078-P01
Of all the thoughts enclosed in the language of Inspiration [[lost: left edge]] its laws its precepts its promises what is there that conveys more, to our minds and hearts than is expressed in this short sentence? Jesus wept. Let us look a moment at the character of Jesus: who, and what is he? If we accept the truths of the Bible, he is the son of God, The King of the Jews, The bright and morning star! A ruler of the Jews came to Jesus and said, "we know thou art a teacher come from God for no man can do the miracles that thou doest, except God be with him." Look then at the power of this man: by his word water is turned into wine. The blind receive their sight, the lame walk. By His word the tempest is stilled, and He walks the waves of the wild Galilee. By His word the dead are called back to life again. Truly He was the son of God.

05-078-P02
But it is not from the Bible alone that we have testimony in regard to the greatness of this man. Renan in his Life of Christ says "All ages will proclaim that among the sons of men there were none born greater than Jesus." His goodness, [[greatness?]], his omnipotent power, we cannot doubt. We stand in awe as we contemplate them, and our hearts acknowledge him as King of Kings and Lord of Lords. But while we contemplate all of this glorious majesty our eye falls upon this simple sentence Jesus wept. What do we learn by this? by these two short words. We learn that He had a heart that could be touched by the sorrows and Suffering of our frail humanity: that Sympath[[lost: torn edge]]

EDITORIAL NOTE: Also reproduced in source 02, scan page 3. Damaged line beginnings and the torn final word remain unresolved. Continues on scan page 79.

====================================================================
05-079 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 79

05-079-P01
the power to "make e'en the wretched blessed" had its abode in His breast and that He could be touched by a feeling of our infirmities. How our hearts thrill with emotions of Love and thankfulness when we think of this blessed truth, that He was in sympathy with the sorrows of frail humanity.

05-079-P02
More than eighteen hundred years have been measured by the revolving Earth since Jesus walked the shores and waves of Galilee, but the influence of His words and life, are still felt. And down through the vista of the coming Ages they will be felt till time shall be no more. And with all the records of His wonderful life this simple truth will descend with the others, Jesus wept.

05-079-P03
L. Douglas.
Sylvana Feb 16th 1883

05-079-P04
Forever reign victorious King
Wide through the earth thy name be known;
And call our longing Souls to sing,
Sublimest anthems near thy throne.

05-081 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 81

05-081-P01
The beauty of female culture.
There is nothing more important than the cultivation of our minds there is given to each of us a mind some superior to others but it matters not what degree of intellect we possess it is the common duty of all to improve it If we do not do this what will be the result? why it will be as a field that is not in cultivation and grows up in bryars and weeds.

05-081-P02
The greater mind we possess the more we are expected to accomplish. I think there is more in the cultivation of the mind than there is in the mind itself.

05-081-P03
We must also cultivate the heart kindness of feelings and gentleness of disposition.

05-081-P04
There is also a great importance attached to the cultivation of our manners for what makes a young lady appear to better advantage than gracefulness and politeness of manners all true politeness originates in kindness of heart for if we feel kindly towards anyone then it is no trouble to act kindly to them.

05-081-P05
Then let us all [[deleted: illegible]] earnestly strive to accomplish this so that we may make ourselves equal with those that now so far surpass us.

05-081-P06
Sarah E. Douglas
April [[21?]].

EDITORIAL NOTE: Essay. Date is faint; no year is visible.

05-083-P01

“Parting from Friends”
by Emma Bell

====================================================================
05-084 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 84

05-084-P01
Parting From Friends
O! how sad we feel when we part from our dear friends! This is the case when we part a short time only, and expect to meet again soon, but how much [[inserted: illegible]] sad it is to part with them when we know that we will meet them no more in this world! Who can discribe the feelings of them who stand around the death bed of a loved father or mother, brother or sister, and hear from their dying lips the last fond words of love they will ever be permitted to utter. And then how agonizing to follow their lifeless bodies, and see them placed in the cold, and silent grave. No one can imagine the feelings caused by such a trial [[deleted: expept]] [[inserted: except]] those who have experienced it. All should be prepared to die, for we know not how soon we may be called upon to leave this beautiful world, and all our dear friends and loved ones. We should ever be kind to our relatives and friends for we do not know how soon they may be taken away from us, and then it will be a sad parting, indeed if we have ever treated them with unkindness.

05-084-P02
[[separate block: annotation below essay]]
Very good

EDITORIAL NOTE: The correction above “sad” is not securely readable. “Very good” appears below the essay in a different-looking hand.

05-087 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 87

05-087-P01
Murfreesboro Tenn
Dec. 6–1883.
Mrs. Douglas and Mrs. Orr.
Ferris, Texas.
Dear Friends:
I send you an advertisement of the land sale which explains itself. During the Term of the Court, Chancellor Burton was sick and died. While he was sick a Circuit Court Judge held his Court and decided the case as to who the land goes to, but seemed very much tangled in the case.

05-087-P02
He decided that 180 Acres of the land that descended to Mrs. Alexander from her father, Jacob Wright, goes to or is inherited by the nephews and nieces of Jacob Wright, and to the children of his deceased nephews and nieces, or their representatives, and as he had about forty nephews and nieces, seventeen of whom are living, and about twenty three dead would make about forty shares into which the estate would be divided. As to the McGlothlin tract of 230 Acres, descended to Mrs. Alexander from her mother, to use his language he said this tract “ascends” to the heirs of Mary Wright from her daughter, the said Rebecca, and the father and mother being cousins, that the heirs of the father and mother of Mary Wright, related to her in equal degree, or representing those in equal degree, take equal moities of said tract of 235 Acres – if such heirs or those they represent

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continues on the following scan. The tract acreage appears as 230 and later 235 on this page; both readings are retained.

====================================================================
05-088 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 88

05-088-P01
do not stand in equal degree to the said Mary Wright then the heirs nearest in blood to the said Mary, or them representing those nearest in blood to her of the whole and half blood will take the whole of said 230 Acres in preference to those of more remote degree.” I appealed from the whole decree to the Supreme Court as also did the McGlothlin claimants of the 230 Acre tract. I am in no way discouraged by the decision of the Court, and feel satisfied that I am correct in my construction of the law that Mrs. Alexander’s first cousins, living at the time of her death, inherit her whole estate from her, and that it does not ascend to the heirs of the Father, or Mother. About the time the case will be finally ended in the Supreme Court, the land money will be ready for distribution.
Very Respectfully,
B. F. Lillard.
Per Ida.

05-088-P02
Criminal and Chancery Courts to day are both in session and I have no time to write.
B. F. Lillard

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continuation of 05-087. Final note and signature are in a different hand from the body, which is marked “Per Ida.”

State of Texas
Ellis County
I Mrs Mary Douglas of said County and State do hereby nominate and appoint B. F. Lillard of Murfreesboro Tenn, my true and lawful Attorney for me and in my name to demand of any person in the possession of the same, the distributive share or interest in the real estate that descended to me from Mrs Rebecca T. Alexander, late of Rutherford County and State of Tennessee, now deceased, I do further empower him to sue for and recover said real estate, and to file a bill for partition, or for sale for partition, giving and granting to said Attorney full power and authority to do and perform every act whatsoever, requisite and necessary to be done in and about the premises, as fully to all intents and purposes as I might or could, if personally present at the doing thereof, hereby ratifying and confirming all that my Attorney may lawfully do, or cause to be done by virtue hereof.
This [[blank]] day of [[blank]] 1883
Witnesses,

05-092-P01
Murfreesboro Feby 26th 1883.
Mr Geo. Douglas
Ferris Texas –
Dear Sir – Mr Lillard our Attorney has shown me a letter from you in answer to two letters that I requested him to write to your Mother and Cousin Cynthia Orr, and in reply to your letter I have this to say. There is no other Attorney representing any of the heirs of Rebecca Alexander and there is no necessity for any other, I have conferred with Cousin Docia Bivins, she stated that she was going to leave her part, I have also conferred with Isaac N. Wright who joined me in employing Mr Lillard to investigate and take charge of the whole matter and we gave him to understand that he would represent all the heirs, I have had letters written to all from a distance, and he has recd a letter from Mrs Nance – which has been answered and we are looking for a Power of Attorney from her, as the whole matter has been explained to her, and she stated that she wanted her part – Isaac Wright recd a letter from his sister Mandy requesting him to act for her. We want all the parties

05-093 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 93

05-093-P01
to join as Complainants in the bill to save costs to the Estate. and we thought it just to give all a chance to unite in the bill – The costs and Attorney’s fees will of course come out of the proceeds of the land and you need have no fears on that, and I assure you that Mr Lillard who is competent and has the reputation of an honest lawyer. and whom I have-known from infancy will treat all parties justly – Mr Alexander has told several parties that he will not litigate the matter – that he would do what was right about it – Our Attorney is running the case as smoothly as possible so as to avoid litigation, but the bill has to be filed to sell the land for division of the proceeds, and to settle the rights of the parties,

05-093-P02

I wish they would return to him the Powers of Attorney immediately – Your Cousin
M M Dill.

05-095 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 95

05-095-P01
Murfreesboro Tenn Dec. 30/82
Mrs Mary Douglas
[[deleted: Ennis]] [[inserted: Ferris]] Texas.
Dear Madame
I suppose that you have heard of the death of your Cousin Mrs Alexander daughter of your uncle Jacob Wright. She did not make a will and her real estate descends to her next of kin consisting of her first Cousins now living being 3 of the Goodloe family, three of the Dill, two of the Brasher and six of the Thompson Wright family as I have been informed by Messrs Wright & Dill, I have been employed by a good many of the heirs to represent them in the matter and at their request I write to you, as it is very desirable that I should be the Attorney for all the interested parties and I want authority to act for you, I have written to Mrs Orr Post Office [[deleted: Ennis]] [[inserted: Ferris]]. Please notify her of the fact. We desire [[inserted: to]] act as smoothly as possible, and when I am clothed with the proper authority I will make the demand for the possession of the real estate consisting of 680 Acres of land assessed at $8500.00 but of course land generally brings double the assessment. [[inserted: also for possession of the 640 Acre tract hereafter referred to]] If possession is not given on demand an ejectment suit will become necessary. After possession a bill will have to be filed for partition or for sale for partition, There may-be more real estate besides the 680 Acre tract, probably 300 Acres in Ark.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Insertion near the lower right runs into the margin. Original address Ennis is corrected to Ferris; the explanation appears on 05-096.

====================================================================
05-096 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 96

05-096-P01
They sold a short time before her death 640 Acres of the land in this County, Please sign the Power of Attorney I send to you and return it immediately, you can have two persons to witness it, but you need not incur any expense to acknowledge before a clerk as it does not have to be registered. A letter from you would have been sufficient authorizing me to represent you but I prefer the paper to be signed that I send to you, Please answer at your earliest Convenience.
Very Respectfully
B. F. Lillard.

05-096-P02
Feby 11th 1883 – The above letter was mailed and directed to Ennis and after 28 days returned to me. I now learn that your Post Office is Ferris – Owing to the delay I hope that you will answer me immediately – The 640 Acres sold as above referred to was sold in order to convert the property as I have been informed for $6400.00 rather a depreciated price on one two and three years credit, the two last payments not due,

05-096-P03
The conveyance is defective – I have made a thorough investigation and am satisfied that the sale can be declared by the Courts null and void and the land can be recovered.
B. F. L.

05-096-P04
[[separate block: lower note]]
Whatever the law gives you is right and if Mrs Alexander had not wanted the land to go to her kin she could have willed it otherwise So she left the law of the land as her will,

05-096-P05
[[margin]]
P. S. If you do not join the others as a Complainant you will necessarily be made a party defendant and under a decision of our Court you being an adult and seeking no relief will take nothing and your share will be lost to you – besides within a few years you would be barred by the Statute of Limitations
[[/margin]]

05-098 | manuscript

05-098-P01
Murfreesboro Tenn. March 22nd 1883
Mr Geo. Douglas
Ferris Texas –
Dear Sir – Your letter of March 15th has been recd. I think that you have been under a misapprehension about several things – In the first place you certainly must think that after I recover these lands that I want to handle the money that may arise from the sale – In this you are mistaken I simply wanted to represent you in the litigation (I mean your Mother & Aunt) I never draw the funds of a party unless specially requested to do so – it is too much trouble to keep receipts &c, but then at the same time I want you to understand that I am thank God far ahead in this world’s goods, and I don’t know of a human being to whom I owe a cent, or whom I ever wronged, You do not understand the law in this State applicable to Attorneys – If an Attorney collects money and fails to pay it over his License [[deleted: was]] [[inserted: is]] taken from him, he is forever prohibited from practicing law, fined a thousand dollars, and imprisoned in jail 12 months, If you remember my first letter to [[deleted: you]] [[inserted: the ladies]] I stated that a letter instructing me to represent them was sufficient, but the Powers of Attorney that I sent was a more formal way and was preferable, and that I did not care about them acknowledging it [[inserted: before a clerk]] [[deleted: illegible]] your first suggested the acknowledgement, showing that

05-099 | manuscript

05-099-P01
that all I desired was simply to do the [[lawing?]] and this was at the request of those whom I represent here, This litigation so far has I regret to say exhibited no small amount of bitterness of soul on the part of some persons, who are not even interested in it, except to try to injure me, and to aid Alexander – They have tried to get some of my clients who live at a distance to discharge me and employ Alexander’s Lawyer [[inserted: & so far have failed]] – Now if they can succeed in getting all the parties who do not reside here on Alexander’s side that are made defendants as I was compelled to place your Mother & Aunt at the time of filing the bills, and then buy out my resident clients of Course the bills would be dismissed – It looks like a blind man ought to see through this – And again Alexander I learn wants to buy the land (which at one time he said was willed to him and now abandoned on account of my persistent efforts against him and others who did not want “to disturb their Cousin Andy or make him mad as they expressed it) and now if, in the bill that I have filed to sell it [[deleted: illegible]] he succeeds in getting most all of the parties on his side, he thinks that he could control to some extent the division of the land into lots, and buy at his own price – Can’t you see into this – These are stubborn facts and I have thoroughly posted you in the whole matter, You have the recommendation of Albert Jetton of me as an Attorney, the editorial of the Free Press that I sent to you, also the certificate of Judge Burton the Chancellor before whom the case will be tried, of John Woods Chairman of the County Court

05-102 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 102

05-102-P01
whisky for neuralgic rheumatism, and like every body else who uses whisky of any temperament, I sometimes used too much, but I never did use it regularly – and now for a great while I have abandoned the prescription, and I do not use any intoxicating drinks, not even berry wine, or cider, as the whole citizens of this Town will testify, and if any man says that I ever neglected my professional engagements on account of whisky he tells that which is absolutely untrue, I do use two cups of Coffee per day, I smoke tobacco and sometimes a little Mullein prescribed by a Methodist Preacher several years ago, but my respect for ladies is too great to allow me to chew tobacco, and this is the extent of my stimulants, Isaac N. Wright, Col. Brashears & Mr Dill & others who reside here are my clients – they have known me from infancy & ought to know what is best, One other party said that she would have nothing to do with it – her and her husband both told me the same, and the latter lately – the other resident party employed a Counsel who came and wanted to join me in the bills, but said he also represented a lot of second Cousins, I told him that his clients interests conflicted, that my bills denied second Cousins having any interests, and that I should comply with the rules of the Bar, that is, when parties originate a suit, and some in interest don’t want to employ their Counsel in a partition, they are made defendants & I would do so in this case, as I did not want to practice Law for persons who did not desire me to – he said he had not investigated the rights of the parties – I replied “that I had” And now I do not desire to take a Lawyer on the cases with me and teach him the law and pay him for the privilege by a division of my fees.
Let me hear from you immediately and if you desire further inquiries I will cheerfully answer you – Very Respectfully B. F. Lillard

05-102-P02
[[margin]] caused from Army wounds [[/margin]]

05-103-P01
P. S. In addition to what I have said I will state that I have been informed by one of your true friends, that you have been written to, to turn your back on me and to employ a certain other Lawyer here – I will now proceed to give you the origin of that – Mr Alexander sent for the old Gentleman to meet him in Murfreesboro one day this week – he did so – they went to the Office of Mr Alexander’s Lawyer, and the three held a long [[deleted: Counsel]] [[inserted: Consultation]], the result of which was, that he went from that Law Office, and consulted with another Lawyer, and then he wrote his letter suggesting to you to employ that Lawyer – Against this Attorney I have not a word to say, nor will I be so uncharitable as to say aught against any brother Lawyer – but no Lawyer is infallible, and a Lawyer is as liable to be imposed [[inserted: on]] as any one else, when the case is stated to him from the opposite side, and that too by an old person whose prejudices can be worked upon – Why did not Mr. Alexander bring into the Consultation your Cousin Albert Jetton whose store is within 75-ft of that Law Office, It seems to me that you ought to be able to see through all this, My bills and work have won the battle unless trickery turns the tide, but I am determined to head off any such warfare, and if any party gives aid and comfort to the enemy to defeat their own rights they shall do so with their eyes open – If Captured by the enemy of this Lawsuit they shall be willing Captures – I further learned that Mr Alexander’s Lawyer said that he could not

I have set out to defeat these parties & I intend to do it, by every thing that an honorable man can do, nor do I intend to permit unjust and wrongful impressions to go out against me, I have as many diplomas from as good Universities as this Country affords, my Law Diploma was signed 25 years ago by Judges Green & Caruthers of Cumberland University, the ablest Jurists Tennessee ever had, and I defy any living man to point to a single stain of dishonor on my character – You yourself ought to be able from what I have informed you to understand all these matters – I would suggest that you lay all of my letters before some good Lawyer of your County, and I know what advice he would give you – There is nothing in this letter intended to reflect on your conduct towards me, Everything is written with the kindest of feelings – because I appreciate fully what has acted on your mind –

05-107 | manuscript

05-107-P01
Murfreesboro Tenn 2–26–83
Mr Geo Douglas
Ferris Texas –
Dear Sir – Your favor of 17th Inst to hand, I enclose you the letter of M. M. Dill which explains most of the matters about which you made inquiry, I would be as free from bringing a suit against any one to defraud them of their just rights as any person who would decline to receive the rewards of such litigation, The 640 Acre Tract of land was conveyed by deed to D Travis for the Consideration of $6400.00 to be paid in three equal payments Augst 1882, 83, 84 – It was not dated but that is obviated by the date of the acknowledgement before the Clerk July 15–1881, by A. M. Alexander – on the same day a Commission was issued by the Clerk to D. M. McKnight a Justice of the Peace and a half brother of A. M. Alexander to take the Privy Examination of Mrs Alexander which he certifies to the next day, This deed was registered Sept, 1882, and she died Nov, 11 1882,

05-107-P02
Now the legal point in the matter is that the Privy Examination of Rebecca Alexander is a Judicial act of the Justice, and he being of kin by affinity within that degree under our Statute [[deleted: the]] privy examination was null and void and the deed conveyed no title to Travis, Now whether there was

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continues on 05-108. Scan is inverted. Historical legal argument transcribed as the writer’s claim.

05-108 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 108

05-108-P01
any agreement between Alexander & wife as to the reinvestment of the proceeds I am not as yet advised – If this deed had been properly executed the sale would have converted the land into personalty and it would be absolutely the husband’s money,

05-108-P02
My remark in my letter was simply the conclusion of one of my clients as to the purpose of the Conversion – One of them related to me that he was at Mr A’s house on one occasion when Mr A. argued the propriety of selling out and purchasing property in Town and appealed to him for approval and he did not seem to like his answer or rather seemed displeased at it, which was against Town property &c, As to fees and the costs we are not allowed as in your State to take Contingent fees, it would be champertous and a suit so taken by an Attorney would be dismissed, but of course Lawyers do not charge so high where there is no recovery – we look to amount involved &c, I can say this much that we will have no Controversy about the fees, and as Mr. Dill’s letter indicates we may avoid litigation, and simply have partition bills, and if it becomes necessary to litigate about the 640 Acre Tract of land I will notify you of all the particulars and await your advice before using the ladies names as Complainants – The parties are very much scattered, and I desire to commence proceedings in time for next Court and hope you will answer me immediately,
Very Respectfully B. F. Lillard.

05-108-P03
[[margin]]
No one will object to demanding of Alexander to turn over to the heirs the land, and when I get the Powers of Attorney I will then be authorized to make the demand for Mrs Orr & Douglas together with the others,

05-115 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 115

05-115-P01
P. S. J. N. Spence the soninlaw of Mrs Bivins informed me that Mr A. M. Alexander about two weeks ago said that if they found no will that he did not want any lawsuit and that he would give up the lands, It is reasonable to suppose that a will conveying such a large estate and disposing of it would not be hid way in a corner – None has as yet been found and you may rest assured that none will be produced,

05-115-P02
Now as to my opinion about the recovery of the 640 Acre Tract of land – I have fully investigated the question and I am fully satisfied that the deed can be declared null and void, but whether the parties will seek to do this is a subject for their own consideration, it is my duty as a Lawyer to point out the rights and it is the clients privilege to exercise it, Mr Alexander is rich outside of this – If he was needy and to take from him the benefits of this 640 Acre tract of land would leave him poor I think the moral question would enter and I am free to confess that I could not very zealously prosecute a suit to recover it – I do not think any such considerations apply – We do not know what induced her to join in the deed, but we do know that for years she had refused to sell it, we further are informed that she said that her Father on his death bed told her that he made no will, and asked her to promise the same and that she said that she had so promised and that she did not want Mr Alexander’s kin to have her property – So the law of the land was her will – If she had desired it she could have determined it otherwise,
I do not even suppose that she knew her personal estate would go to her husband, and the taking of her Privy Examina

EDITORIAL NOTE: Postscript appears before the main opening of this letter in the scan order. Continues mid-word on 05-116. The reported deathbed conversation is a secondhand account.

====================================================================
05-116 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 116

05-116-P01
tion by a kinsman that she executed the deed freely and voluntarily and without the constraint of her husband certainly is a circumstance to arouse suspicion to say the least of it, But all these matters are subjects for the heirs to think about, I have been informed that a few of the heirs say that they will have none of the property, but against some of these no Statute of Limitation runs as two of them have living husbands, besides Mr Dill thinks that there are certain business relations existing between them and Mr. A. and that after he and others proceed to the recovery or get everything proposed then they will come in – so as not to incur the displeasure of Mr. A, These facts I write to you confidentially as I desire you to be thoroughly posted as to everything going on here, So far as I am individually responsible as an Atty, I suppose Mr A. Jetton & Mr Dill satisfies you as to that, and then you agree that all ought to have the same Attorney. I will expect to get the Powers of Attorney – Of Course it would be better for the Powers of Attorney to be acknowledged before a Notary Public – but as I stated before any written authority will do to bring the suits or file the bills –
Very Respectfully
B. F. Lillard

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continues the word “Examination” split at the boundary from 05-115. The legal claims are attributed to the writer. Scan is inverted.

05-119 | manuscript

05-119-P01
1870
[[11?]]
Readyville Tenn Feb 20th
Mrs Sue Bell
My very Dear Cousin
I seat myself this cold Sabath Morning to answer your kind and interesting letter which I received in due time I would have written before now but have been very Sick and am just getting able to get about again Dr. Briges who Attend me during my sickness Thinks My illness was brought on by cold which effected my whole nervous Sistim I never Suffered so much in my life and am still very feble

05-119-P02
The rest of the family are all very well and getting on finely This cold weather Aunt Clark has been with us several days but is going to leave to morrow

05-120-P01
if the [[inserted: weather]] is not to bad It is Thought The Legislature will Adjurn by the 15th of march And this may be her last visit to us We are all very sorry to give her up as She has been with us so much She seems like one of the family We will be very lonesome when she is gone. Sallie Wieden is also spending a few days with us She tells us that She has had Three [[Divorces?]] but is as lively as a cricket And thinks She would like to marry again if She could get a chance The kin are all Tolerable well at This time Johns wife has another fine boy Three weeks old Johns health is good as usual. Old Aunt Polly Wright died about two weeks ago She died I suppose with old age I have not heard [[Saboth?]] any Thing in [[Bras?]] family lately I suppose they are all well or we would hear [[illegible]]

05-120-P02

You want to know all about all of the girls who they married and how they are getting on Well Sarah married a drunken trifling fellow by the name of Tullison They have been married fifteen years and have eight living children They are living on a rented place not far from [[Mam?]] Bras Tullison drinks up nearly all he makes And his family have a very hard road to [[weed?]] Smith Married a man name Popley he does not drink but is so lazy he hardly makes a support for his family [[Temith?]] is very sickly She reminds of a bag of cotton with a string tied around it She has only two children Marier is the looking and the smartest one of the family She married [[Darnum?]] [[Vaught?]] he was killed shortly after we came here in a drunken spree Marier has four children Two boys large enough to work She is living at her fathers

05-123 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 123

05-123-P01
Cross Creek Travis Texas
August the 30th 1869

05-123-P02
George & Lizzie, your letters were duly and gladly rec, for I am ever anxious to hear from home, or Brushy and its vicinity — I've nothing very interesting to offer at present. The weather is extremely hot and dry in this section since the ((flood)) as I term it and in consequence a great deal of sickness below this Mostly Chills and fever

05-123-P03
I was riding, three days last week trying to rent Me a place nearer Austin But failed All occupied for another Year. and the people not all But a great many sick for the season

05-123-P04
I have become a little dissatisfyed with the Mountains Oweing to the roughness of the road and distance from Austin I was fully determined to move to Arkansas this fall. But I must Confess that the grief and tears of a good Old motherinlaw is a preventative, at least as long as She lives and possibly She may out live Me.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Letter continues on 05-124–125. Image rotated for reading.

====================================================================
05-124 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 124

05-124-P01
My grass was a total loss; corn ½ rotten my Cotton I thought at first was not injured But time tells it, I think maybe one third of a crop now all considered has a tendency to get a man down in the mouth a little But continuing in the same strain of Eloquence

05-124-P02
[[deleted: illegible]] tell Tom and Laura that Dubly Beats Me, But I must say that on the second of August Earl Stewart Douglas made his appearance Weighing ten pounds he is growing finely and [[Ma?]] has been up two weeks doing her work. I think that is enough to raise a mans ambition above rotten Corn mark — ((what you say)) not knowing exactly where I will get to mail this I will close for the present.

05-124-P03
Sept the 15th I seat Myself to add a few more lines, all been sick a little, though all well at present But Earl and he is improving. I have been gathering Corn [[deleted: for a]] few days (not half done) Will make or have 300 hundred Bushels sound Corn to My Share 150 or 200 rotten and half rotten Corn which

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continuation of 05-123. The manuscript reads Stewart; the later identifying slip 05-122 reads Stuart. “300 hundred” is retained as written. Continues on 05-125.

05-125 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 125

05-125-P01
will do the hoggs a little good

05-125-P02
If nothing happens I will start to the City in the morning, will Mail this then in order that you will get it a day or two sooner

05-125-P03
I fear from the progress of my Cotton in the way of its opening I wont make more than 1 Bag. McCluer off of about Eight Acres has already picked Between 5 and 6 Thousand so you see that the Overflow Makes against Me. There is still a gooddeal of sickness in the Country. No news, only the people will Marry down in these parts. dont know how they are to live. But that is their business not mine.

05-125-P04
As I have worked hard to day I will Close for this time write soon

05-125-P05
Yours in haste
Jesse Douglas

05-125-P06
tell Sarah I am coming to see her this Winter or fall

05-130-P04

05-130-P05
I've not put up My hoggs yet wanting them to get the Benefit of the mast as much as possible But I expect to pen this week.

05-131 | manuscript

05-131-P01
George

05-131-P02
It is out of the question Me to move during the Old Stewarts life time I think She would go crasy. She is old and frail in body and mind, yet She may live longer than younger ones. I am not Shure whether I will remain on this place next year or not. a couple of months will decide that. As I Cant Mo[[lost: torn edge]] from this Country I will Leave [[lost: torn edge]] to make your figures In regard the Old Farm

05-131-P03
The Overflow and sickness given Me [[illegible: approximately 3 words]] in the way of a living, and [[lost: torn edge]] a going to take some time to [[lost: torn edge]] a Start again, there is a prospect of My moving to McCluers [[Neibors?]] which will Cut Me Out of My present Stock of hoggs I could not move them as they would come run Back to the mountains Should I leave them here some person Else would eat them. A moving Stuff goes for nothing But a Buyer has to pay the biggest prices

Thence N. 60° E 475 vs. to the beginning containing 82 acres more or less, To have and hold the same together with all and singular, the rights title privileges and appurtenances thereunto belonging forever, and hereby bind myself, my heirs and assigns to forever warrant and defend the title to the same unto the said Lizzie Douglas, her heirs and assigns forever, against all parties claiming the same or any part thereof in through or by me. Provided however that I am to retain the use and control of the described land and its tenements during the period of my natural life. In Witness whereof

06-013-P01
Readyville March 20 1883
Miss Lizzie Douglas
My Dearest Cousin
Your very kind letter was recieved in due time and I was very glad to learn that you were all well and know that you have not forgoten me. Willie & myself are both as well as usual The Kin & friends are tolerble [[inserted: well]] at present. Mary Lees Babe little Granville has been very sick but is better to day Paps health has improved very much I think he looks [[inserted: now]] better than he has in Several months Adele spent Several days with me last week she is looking well and is very much pleased with her new home Bro Mcguffy has moved to his new home they have a very nice house & Yard and are

EDITORIAL NOTE: Letter continues on scans 14–15. The year is positioned above the date.

06-014 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 14

06-014-P01
much pleased with their Surroundings I am very glad to have Jennie So near me it is only a half a mile to her house and we visit each other often Aunt Sallie is still in west Tenn I dont [[inserted: know]] whether She will come back this Spring or not we [[inserted: have]] not heard from her in Some time I [[inserted: have]] been gardening Some but we have had So much Cold weather our vegatables are not doing much good we had quite a Snow Storm yest but the Snow is all gone this morning and it is very pleasant out of doors in the Sun I have Such a lonely time here only a little negro with me Willie is at home of nights but gone every day he is working land over at his Gran Paps this year and is either over there or off with his Waggon hauling for

06-014-P02
Some one [[inserted: all the time]] he never misses a day if he Can help it So you See I am very much confined at home and consequently very lonely. You wished to know the particulars of Cousin Rebeccas Alexanders death I did not know She was Sick until the night before She died I was at the funeral and learned there that She exposed herself getting up very early of mornings to look at the Comet which She was very much interested about She took a very Severe Cold and died from the effect of it I think She was in her right mind all the time during her Sickness Sat up eat her breakfast and talked with her friends in the morning and died that evening a few a Clock, As to the Lawsuit I know very little about it only you know just what I hear [[inserted: hearsay]] other People Say

EDITORIAL NOTE: Two facing pages, transcribed left then right. The account of Rebecca Alexander’s illness and death is the writer’s report, not a medical determination.

====================================================================
06-015 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 15

06-015-P01
The latest and most reliable I have heard is from Charlie he and Pap were at Cousin Andys last Satterday they found him in very poor health and in a great deal of Trouble he told them Cousin Rebecca will was stolen out of her draw two weeks before her death he is willing to give up the land does not wish to have a law Suit but wants to buy the land of the heirs has already made arangements to get the interest of Several of the Wright family The Jacob Wright Estate is said to be worth about $[[26000?]] and my own opinion is the heirs ought to have it as Cousin Andy will be a rich man without it My best love to you all write soon
Most affectionately your Cousin
L A Jetton

06-015-P02
[[margin]]
Lizzie please dont let any one read this poor Scribled letter I am ashamed of it but have got so out of the habit of writing I cant write fit to be seen
[[/margin]]

06-017 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 17

06-017-P01
Readyville Tenn March the 3 1878
Miss Lizzie Douglas
My very dear Cousin
Your kind and interesting letter came to hand in due time and I hasten to reply. This leaves the family in rather poor health, Pap & Mother are both Complaining and the Children all have the Whooping Cough. Willie has it very bad and besides that he has had a spell of billious fever is sitting up a little this morning for the first time in Several days. Adele is quite sick with the Cough She has it the second time. Cousin Nellie is getting better but Charles Children are very bad off. Well the wedding Came off at the appointed time we had nice Crowd a good supper and every thing passed off quietly. Adele Willie and myself all went to the reception At Cousin Miriams they had a splendid dinner and all seemed to enjoy it very much Jimmie & Mary are going to live with Cousin Miriam as She is almost alone having only one Child single and he is hardly ever at home Jimmie and Mary Came over yes evening they are both well they have [[inserted: gone]] to Church to day at newhope We had Some fun out of them this morning

06-017-P02
[[margin]]
you must excuse all errors as the Children have kept up such a noise and talking I could hardly write at all will try to do better next time
Yours as ever
L A J
[[/margin]]

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continues on scan 18. The postscript is inverted above the opening date.

====================================================================
06-018 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 18

06-018-P01
about their kin they both call their motherinlaws Cousin they say the kin is so mixt up they dont [[inserted: know]] how to claim it So they will just call them all the same they did before they were married. Well Lizzie Jimmie was a boy sixteen years old when we came here and the first time I met him I told may beth I had picked him out for my Soninlaw (just in fun you know) She was very indignant about it vowed She wouldnt have him I laugh at her [[deleted: now]] [[inserted: now]] about [[deleted: her]] Changing [[inserted: her]] mind she says people often do that it is their privledge The kin and friends are generaly well so far as I know We have had a great deal of bad rainey weather this winter we hardly ever [[inserted: have]] more than [[one or?]] good days at a time The farmers are getting discouraged about not getting to plough any Wheat Crops looks very fine. Dear Cousin you have my deepest sympathy in your great afflictions I loved Cousin Sue on earth I hope to meet her in heaven where Sorrows and partings are unknown I [[inserted: will]] have to Close for the present Tell uncle Jimmy Mary is going to write to him before long and Send him hers & Jimmies pictures Give my best love to the kin and please accept a large share your self I want you to write to me as often as you can as I am always so glad to get a letter from you Adele says tell [[Cousin S he?]] will write to her before long when She gets well of the whooping Cough the Children all send much love to you
most affectionately your cousin
L A Jetton

06-024 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 24

06-024-P01
Readyville Tenn Feb. 1 1874
Miss Lizzie Douglas
Dear Cousin your very kind and truly welcome letter was recieved in due time and I now hasten to reply This leaves us all very well at present and the kin and friends are all well so far as I know The health of the naberhood has been very good this winter We have had a very mild winter so far a great deal of rain and very little snow The young people have had a gay time of it here this winter more parties and big dinners than I ever heard off The Children were at two last week one at Charlies and another at Bells but I hope they are through for the present as they are going to Start to school next week and will have to Stop runing about The school will be taught at Readyville by one of our preaching brethren he has been teaching for many years and is a Splendid scholar and a good man We have preaching at newhope once a month Bro Rice Sewell preaches for us again this year We have preaching at three other churches

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continues on scan 25.

====================================================================
06-025 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 25

06-025-P01
on different days so we can go every Sunday if we like Mr Barr and Aunt Jane Came up and Spent a night with us last week they were both very well and their Children were all well Aunt Jane is very much broken and look old and feeble Mr Barr looks pretty much the same has for the last five years. Marybee went to Readyville last week and [[deleted: illegible]] her picture taken to send to [[obscured: smudge]] you She was going to write to you this evening but some of her beaux coming in She will have to defer writing untill an other time Well Cousin I was very much surprised when I saw the Money you sent me I never dreamed of such a thing as your Ma selling the saddle but as you say money never comes amiss I was very glad to get it. Charlie and family are all getting on finely Little Alma is a sweet little thing and talks quite plain She favors her gran ma very much Charlie is Still my Bro and friend I would go see him often poor fellow but I have less use for his lady than any woman I ever saw though I try to be friendly with her for his sake but I have said to much already and will Stop you must write soon and tell me all the news give my love to all the kin tell them if I live I am coming to see them I must bring the Children all send much love to you I will close as it is late
Ever your friend and cousin
L A Jetton

06-025-P02
[[margin]]
Mr Eddie Jolly one of Emmas brothers is coming to see Marybee I dont know what Peter will say about it

06-034 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 34

06-034-P01
Saturday 19th Lizzie I expected to write a good deal sooner than I have but since I stoped the other night circumstances have been such that it would be inconvenient to do Grand ma was taken very sick night before last and was quite sick yesterday though is up this morning Bud John had a chill night before last but I hope as the weather is getting cool we will not be troubled much with chills.
I didn't know until a little while ago that one of the family would be going to town to day so I will have to close hurriedly—I expect to have company this winter Aunt Jane and her family (Jennys own mother) expect to move out in a few weeks. her husband and two of her daughters and their families have gone to California Uncle J. has only gone to look at the country if he likes it he will move Aunt J. and the children out They are coming to stay here until he returns or sends for them. So will have two young lady Cousins with us and little one enough to keep music all the time I reckon it will be right hard for us to get used to the noise of Children we have been living so quiet the boys in the field the most of the time leaving Grandma and me alone. I'm very anxious to go to school this winter but will not be able to do so I want Pa to send the little boys if possible for they are getting to be big boys

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continues on scan 35. The month and year are not given in the opening date.

06-040 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 40

06-040-P01
Mosshead Ala.
Sept 15th 1868
My dear Lizzie
After so long, long a silence I will [[obscured: ink blot]] now endeavor to converse a little with you through the medium of my pen. What is the cause of this silence? Am I to blame? if I am I am sorry for it But did I not answer your last letter? It has been so long since I have had the pleasure of receiving a letter from you that I cant say whether I answered it or not (I would not have you think me indifferent about it but I received one from you while Pa was sick and I had so little time to write in that I have really forgotten whether I replied or not, I read your letter over but couldn't tell [[inserted: from that]] At any rate I have been waiting for a reply but as it doesn't come I am trying to see if this sort of scribling can call forth a few words from you
I feel like I have many things to say to you but my mind is wandering to night and I dont know that I can get it sufficiently [[inserted: concentrated]] to write any thing so that you can understand it As I hardly know [[illegible]] what first I will begin

06-040-P02
[[margin]]
Each form that the providence of God to his people &c
[[/margin]]

06-041 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 41

06-041-P01
with the health of the family and friends There has been a great deal of sickness in the community but we have been richly blessed Pa had one right severe spell but recovered from it before the sickliest part of the season came and Charlie had a light spell of fever which lasted only a few days with this exception we have escaped, that is, our immediate family. Cousins C. & L. Walker came out to spend some time with us and were both taken sick at once and stayed very sick for about two weeks They were carried to town as soon as they were able Since then they have been very sick, since they left, there was about a week that I missed but two evenings from visiting the sick Bud J & D have gone to sit up with a sick neighbor tonight. About a month ago I saw something that I never witnessed before—three corpses at one sight all to be laid in one family grave yard Cousin [[M. W.?]] and her little infant were laid in one coffin and Mrs. G's little girl in another The coffins were set close together with the lids off so that all three might be seen at a glance while the preacher stood at the head to perform the last religious rite: happy trio! Cousin [[inserted: M W]] was a devout Christian and the two little ones had never known sin Cousin M. left [[inserted: eight]] [[deleted: illegible]] children—[[two?]] grown live

less, six girls, the oldest in her fifteenth year The oldest boy (about sixteen) left home a few days before her death for Virginia to go to school poor boy! to be sad to think that the first tidings he would hear from home would be of the death of the Mother whom he left well and whose idol he was. Poor Cousin! he wrote to know if she mentioned him in her last hours But her energy [[conceals?]] [[illegible]] she neither spoke of death or of her children but she left them an example though short was her morning [[illegible]] if we can judge; she was prepared.
I will now tell you something of our being teachers. I have a class in Sunday School composed of seven boys the oldest I suppose about fifteen I am becoming very much interested in them But oh! the responsibility To think that what I teach them will perhaps direct the course of their lives! It will either assist in leading them to Christ or it will take them farther from him for “He that does not gather with me scattereth abroad” The oldest of them has had very little teaching the first Sunday he came into the class I told him where his next lesson was he said he didn't have any book “well” said I “you have a book at home

06-045 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 45

06-045-P01
Mosshead Ala. Monday
Sept 30th 1867
My Dear Lizzie
Your kind and welcome letters of the 31st of Aug. and 1st of Sept reached me about a week ago I hope you will not think hard of me for not answering it sooner I had an opportunity of doing so but didn't want to write to you & Mollie at the same time as you generally hear from each others letters
It seems that you think from my letters that I am a great complainer indeed I have had great reason to complain it is only by complaining that I get letters you and Annie Roberts have been more punctual than any of my correspondents you are very charitable for keeping my letters on hand so long without answering it as sickness was your excuse I dont think I am unreasonable in my complaints when my correspondents render a good excuse I am always willing to forgive but there is some thing strange about it as much as I complain I have never had any of them to complain of me I conclude it is because I dont deserve it
[[illegible]] much more lively but it has been sickly last week we were all able to be up at one time but we couldn't hold out at that some body had to be sick so Frank and I both took chills I did not but one but have been feeling badly every other day Frank still continues to have them had one last night and has high fever now he has a hard time of it has been sick a great deal this summer I am afraid it will get us in to hard spells of fever Bud Davy has a chill

06-045-P02
[[margin]]
send me your Photograph when you have it [[illegible]]
[[/margin]]

06-048-P01
Bible through I am afraid he will not do it though I keep encouraging him Although we are in [[illegible]] and have nothing like methodist preaching and Methodist doctrines yet I think I have a greater influence over my brothers now than I ever did and I feel the weight of the responsibility of it Oh! may God assist me to set a right example I think Grand Ma [[tries?]] hopes to win us all over to the Methodist church she seldom speaks of our difference in belief when she does it is in the kindest manner it is now so dark I can hardly see how to write May be after supper I will tell you something of my evening adventures untill then I will stop writing

06-048-P02
Well it is now nearly bed time and I will resume my writing I said I would tell you something of my evening adventures probably I would have finished this letter this evening but there was a call down stairs for me Mr. — (“my beau”) was there, he asked me to take a ride with [[inserted: him]] and Oh! we had a “[[time of it?]]!” rode on horse back we went to a persimmon tree not a great way off but the most of the persimmons were green we [[deleted: did]] looked for some muscadines we found the “vines” but not the “[[wines?]]” it was late enough in the evening to be pleasant riding I was glad to get the chance (but didn't let him know it) I think it will do me good to ride out once in a while I have stayed at home so much with the sick and been sick so much my self Mr. — said he wished very much he could form the acquaintance of some of my young lady friends in Texas I dont remember what reply I made Dont you wish you could form his acquaintance? my kin ones tease me a great deal about him but there is nothing serious between us and not likely to be so [[illegible]] he is a very nice young man but he doesn't “suit my style” so much of my nonsense

06-048-P03
Crops are tolerably good here corn is turning out pretty well Cotton better than I expected Bud Davy had a chill today but he and Frank were both up late this evening Pa went to Montgomery [[deleted: illegible]] [[inserted: to day]] Tuesday and I was very much surprised at not getting a letter or two he expects to go to Lowndes tomorrow & turn over and I will send this by him to mail there I intended writing you another half sheet at odd times but I do not know when there will be any passing to that again Lizzie please correct all the mistakes you see in my letters and tell me of them and I will do likewise by yours if you wish
As ever your true friend Lizzie Graves

Wednesday night Aug. 29th 1867
Dear Lizzie
Your very welcome letter of the 25th July reached me about two weeks ago I read it while in bed I have been quite sick was in bed nearly a [[illegible]] it [[illegible]] caused from billiousness being sick when I got your letter and very busy since I got well I hope you will excuse me for not answering it sooner I have only partially regained my strength I am greatly reduced in flesh and very much reduced in color My sickness has served to make me look like an Ala. girl the first morning that I was able to fill my seat at the table some of them said “Cousin Lizzie your sickness shows right smartly on you” I said “Oh! it is only helping to make an Ala. girl of me” it seemed like they were going to [[inserted: quiz]] me they all commenced “yes you never have any sickness in Texas” said I “we dont have as many pale faces there as you do here” I was sick two weeks last summer but it didn't take

06-050-P02
[[separate block: left-hand page; concluding page of this letter]]
it out I would have liked so much to have gone I dont know whether there is an organized church and regular preaching down there or not if there is I hope we will get to go yet I havnt met up with any one yet who is a member of the Christian Church Pa and I are alone it makes me feel right badly I do wish there were a church in this neighborhood or even if it were not in this neighborhood if it were near enough for us to attend once in a while that would be better than not to go at all we have very good Methodist preaching here as there are none but Methodists living here we dont have series of doctrinal sermons like they do at Liberty
Lizzie do you ever hear anything from Mr. Goodman these days poor man I reckon he will move and move until he settle in his grave and leave his family helpless it is a pity that he cant be contented to stay in one place I feel sorry for him and family Where is Mr. Austin and what is he doing any thing? he is very much related to Mr. goodman Lizzie I have written a letter to Jane and Mary which I hope you will pardon me for sending in yours give it to any of or all the boys and they will get some person to read it for them you can read it if you want to before you give it to them I hardly knew how to begin or how to end please write me a long letter I have written two [[illegible]] this contains more to write with much love yours Lizzie

EDITORIAL NOTE: Two facing pages. The opening is on the right and continues on scan 51, left. Scan 51, right, continues onto the left-hand concluding page transcribed here. The concluding request to pass along a letter is historical source text, not an action requested of the reader.

06-051-P01
me so long to get my color back but enough of this I wish very much I could have been with you at Mr. Ba's examination not that I think I could have enjoyed the examination and exhibition much but I would have enjoyed your company I have never attended one of his examinations yet but have heard a great deal [[lost: holes]] about them [[lost: hole]] you would have enjoyed being with you at Mr. Horns I think you must be coming out but that is nothing more than might be expected. I have heard that he was trying to shine before one of the city girls who is it? I didn't learn her name
I would have enjoyed helping you with your work—a part of it—I didnt use to love to work with honey much but as this is a honey less country I reckon it would be quite pleasant now I dont think I would have enjoyed washing and ironing much I have plenty of that here—ironing I dont bother washing it would have been delightful to be with you scrubbing the floors I always did love that and have helped do it often

06-051-P02
You said you cut Mollie out you also said you had made a great conquest lately I do wish you would call names I am very anxious to know who the “Sympathizer” is and how I would get paid up “Sympathizers” when there was plenty of “old and new wine” on hands. I thought I was about to make a conquest but some body shined brighter than I and he doesnt know me now and I dont care may be I can eclipse some body some of these days when I get to being “pale and interesting” like all the “rest” of the Ala girls
Lizzie last Saturday evening I was so much delighted I thought I was going to get to hear some good preaching Pa heard of a christian Church some twelve or fifteen miles south of this Saturday evening he asked who wanted to go with him to hear a Christian preacher I told him I did he didn't know the way but Mr. Thurgeson told him he would go with him I made arrangements to go but Sunday morning Pa's eyes were hurting him so bad and it looked so much like rain we had to give

06-053 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 53

06-053-P01
Mosshead May 10th 1867
Dear Lizzie
I expect some of the family will go to town tomorrow and I will try and scratch you a few lines I would have written before now but I received Mollie's letter about two weeks ago she said you had received my letter I have been looking for the reply I had much rather answer a letter than to write before receiving one I heard last week that there were letters in the office for me but when we sent for them [[deleted: illegible]] we couldn't get any So I supposed one of them was from you so I will try and write you something if only enough to bring you in debt to me you will therefore excuse this uninteresting letter for I know I shall fail to interest you as there is no news afloat and but little family news none of the Cousins married yet and not probable that any of them will be soon we are all well at this time except Pa he has had sore eyes very bad but they are improving now I very much feared he would entirely lose one of them at least but now they are so much better I hope he will save both of them When I wrote to Mollie last I was not quite well had two or three chills but broke them up with red pepper

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continues on scan 54. The final word is faint; the description of the remedy is historical wording, not advice.

06-054 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 54

06-054-P01
I have been quite well since then Brothers are now at work trying to earn their bread by the sweat of their brow work seems to agree with Frank [[Thaddie?]] & Charles they grow like all weeds there has been so much [[illegible]] they have had to work harder than they otherwise would have done It rains here nearly every monday and it is not confined to Monday alone it frequently rains nearly all the week
I spent quite a pleasant day yesterday Mrs. W— her daughter and Miss E— spent the day with us they are [[deleted: Though]] very sociable nice people the only family in the neighborhood that have visited us at all
There was a May party given by the ladies at Tusk[[illegible]] the first of this month (or a very [[illegible]]) to decorate the graves of the Alabama confederate dead A few days after the white ladies had enjoyed their [[deleted: illegible]] on the confederate dead The colored ladies of the city of Montgomery formed a procession marched down to the graveyard loaded with flowers to decorate the graves of the Yankee dead Montgomery is quite a fast place they had quite a time decorating the church last Christmas The church convention is now in [[illegible]] for the purpose of uniting the Methodist Episcopal and Methodist Protestant churches again I dont know how they will make it I hope they will all

06-054-P02
be pleased The Catholics have been making quite a [[illegible]] here lately have drawn very considerably from the Methodist Church so much so that some of them are beginning to get alarmed for fear that they will break up their congregation I havnt heard them preach yet Lizzie I hope you will excuse this short letter (also mistakes) I am not in a writing mood tonight as you will see May be I will have time to write some in the morning before Bud Joe gets ready to start he is going to carry corn off for sale it is selling at $2.00 per bushel)
Please write me a long letter in return for this I hope you the time of night which is time to go to bed Good bye
Your true friend Lizzie Graves
Saturday morning I will not have time to write much at all this morning Pa's eyes still improving Give my kind regards to the Dr. and Ellen also to Maggy and Jennie Bell and to all enquiring friends I have not time to call names please write soon and often and long letters in haste and with love L. J. G.

06-056 | manuscript

06-056-P01
Mosshead [[deleted: Thursday]] [[inserted: Friday]] night
Nov. 20th 1868
My dear Lizzie
I commence a letter to you tonight, not knowing when I will finish it. I have made several attempts to reply to your letter of the 11th Oct. but there has always been something to prevent my carrying out my design I have been very busy, and then, we have such a large family, that we never have a quiet moment until bed time. and then I do not feel like writing [[deleted: illegible]]
I reckon you have heard of the additions to Grandma's household—my Aunt & family at first [[deleted: the]] noise of so many children annoyed me, but now I am used to it I reckon I will hardly know what to do with myself when they go away. My Uncle has returned from California highly delighted with every thing—the fruit is the finest he ever saw, the land is the richest and most productive he ever saw and indeed every thing is rather superior to any thing he

06-056-P02
[[margin]]
I have not heard of Mr Winnifords marriage I know how to sympathize with you in your loneliness & I reckon you will know how to sympathize with me in mine when all the children leave this place
I am getting so cold I will have to quit whether I want to or not & I fear means not to quit excuse me for closing my letter so abruptly I expect you will find a good many mistakes but I know I shall try [[illegible]] your letters are always so interesting [[illegible]]
[[/margin]]

06-057 | manuscript

06-057-P01
ever saw. Sanfrancisco is far a head of Montgomery in the fashions if not ahead of New York a person has to be rich to move in the first circle there My cousins found it too gay for them, so they moved about 500 five hundred miles south of that to Los angeles, it is a very quiet and flourishing place. Uncle J. says he is very anxious to move his family out there, but is not able, but I think the Earth quakes they have been having out there have shaken his determination more than any thing else, he has bought a home in Greenville, about thirty miles from here, and they will all move from here in a week or two you know I will be lonesome I have had three room mates all the time, and to be left alone all at once I will feel it very sensibly, but I will have plenty to keep me busy and I expect the time will pass very rapidly
Lizzie I was so glad to get your letter I had felt a good deal like scolding but you are so good to acknowledge your wrong and make such a good promise that I will not quarrel with

06-057-P02
you this time especially as I have been so slow about answering it I must say though that I dont think your excuse for not writing was a very good one, viz, that I was enjoying my self so well you didn't think you could increase my enjoyment I never expect to enjoy my self so much that it would not be increased by a letter from home. Cousin Mary and I have had a good deal of pleasure, riding horse back since the working season has been over, that is generally our resort for pleasure We have been having a good many “beaus” since Cousin M. came Last Sunday was a week, we had four at once, and such a time as we did have one of them would say “yes” or “no” to every thing one said, and one would only agree with us in every thing we said, and one would try to tease us about every thing we said, and the other one seemed to want to say every thing he knew, (which seemed to be very little) I felt like I would rather they had stayed at home and let me spend the evening alone last saturday the one that loved to tease so well [[deleted: illegible]] and really the choicest of the four came

06-058-P01
driving up in his buggy & called for the young ladies Cousin M. & I went in to see him we sat and entertained him very pleasantly all the evening (it was late when he came) but he was not Satisfied with that he stayed all night so all of us young folks sat in the parlor together after supper, and spent “that time” very pleasantly [[illegible: along fold]] Sunday morning he took Cousin M. to church & of course came back here to dinner. Soon as we got through with dinner M. ran off up stairs and left me to talk to him, and do you think she would come down at all? and there poor me had to entertain him but my Pa & my grown brothers being from home, you [[deleted: illegible]] can think before he left he stayed till nearly dark That is what I call [[deleted: illegible]] not only a visit but a “visitation”
Monday morning I had a beau all to myself M. was in school (she is teaching the children at home) (we had a young lady and gentleman to call on us yesterday evening) When they all go away I will not be bothered with the “criturs” and I will be glad of it they are troublesome things to have I reckon it is bed time so I must quit for tonight Good bye

I have a horrible tale to narrate this week tis the murder of Mrs Claunch by her daughter. Mrs C is a sister to Mr [[Simons?]] [[was here in the spring?]] [[illegible]] creek. It seems that they quarreled a few days before when the girl run away and went to [[illegible]] near Fathers, but he got mad and went back home. The children had all gone out to the water melon patch except this girl [[inserted: illegible]] aged 15 years and small child aged 3 year. Her husband had gone to hunt cattle. The evidence is [[illegible]] there was [[illegible]] a severe struggle between them, [[illegible]] was that the girl showed no [[illegible]] of bruises. The girls evidence comes first. She says she was in the [[illegible]] house, and heard her Mother scream [[illegible]] a [[deleted: illegible]] [[illegible]] run in and [[illegible]] her Mother dead [[illegible]] run off and stood by the [[illegible]] while [[illegible]]. [[illegible]] bail. three days was given her to give [[five?]] hundred dollars bond she could not so her [[illegible]] went to [[Wax?]] jail. 06-066 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 66

06-066-P01
have any doubt but what they will take ours. It is now currently reported and believed, that the wheat which the Farmers carried in before the tythe tax, was due, in order that the Soldiers might get some flour has been sold by speculators. They say they would manage it this way; they would [[store?]] it [[illegible]] where a board would be appointed to examine it. They would condemn it as inferior flour, and it would be sold, they would then sell it at an enormous price and pocket the proceeds. There was a man here the other day from Nacochitoches who says this is so, and that Magruder is at the head of it. One thing I know, that a great deal of wheat has been ground up and taken off; and I reckon you could testify, that the Soldiers dont get much of it. What if old Magruder orders you away from Sabine about the time Joda gets back, and takes all of your flour and bacon away from you? We are going to send you a sack of flour a piece and a good supply of fat bacon which I hope will serve to make your fish a little more savory.

06-066-P02
[[illegible]] do live on such poor diet and then to think it is all on account of the Speculators!

06-066-P03
[[margin]] [[illegible]] and [[illegible]] all [[/margin]]

06-069 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 69

06-069-P01
Brushy April 21st 1865
Dear George:

06-069-P02
We were somewhat surprised last tuesday evening — the 18th by the arrival of Joda; we were all truly glad to see him you may be sure. I was a little disappointed in not getting a letter from you, but suppose you have written by mail since your return from Velasco. I am anxious to hear something about Dock, and the something you would tell of next time which you spoke of in yours of the 5th inst. has accomplished its purpose — excited my curiosity. The thread you sent by J. M. was very acceptable, it will save me many a turn of my “Piano.” The pins — although I had plenty you left before — do not come amiss. I have a little speculation in my head, which, if I was sure it would turn out right, I would write you several little articles to buy that you might keep your eyes open for such bargains: but, as I am not sure of accomplishing it, I will just tell you my plan. It is to send down some flour by Joda that Laura and I have. There is only six hundred pounds of it, and Joda says if he gets Transportation at all, he will take it. But he does not know yet whether he will get any thing to transport, untill he hears from Mr Bell. If he takes a waggon we will try to send you a little something to sweeten your tooth. I would like for you to look around a little, and see if a side saddle could be got in Houston. Laura is very anxious to get one, and if the flour speculation succeeds she might stand a chance to get one, as Joda says waggons are continually passing from Houston to Dallas

====================================================================
06-070 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 70

06-070-P01
I recd a letter from Laura this evening. she seems to be very well satisfied in her new situation. says she has from 16 to 19 schollars, with a prospect for more. I suppose it is better than to be carding and spinning but take me from friends and Home, and place me among strangers, and I would feel that I was left to pine neglected, and to “perish in forgetfulness” — to be poetical. Cousin Mollie has just returned from school and is going to stay all night with me so I must quit.

06-070-P02
Saturday evening 22nd Today has been bright and clear, quite an uncommon thing: the wind has been from the north since day before Yesterday, and last night we had frost enough to kill most of our beans, though I do not think the fruit is injured. Cousin M. amused me last night by relating some of her school exploits, and altogether the time was passed off quite pleasantly. She is going to Liberty; A mrs. Bishop is teaching there now. She is from Dallas, and some rather quiter, I mean — could teach almost every thing the Dead Languages included. But May says it is with difficulty she can find the answers in old Kirkham, so I suppose the Living have become Dead to her. Some foul play has been carried on, up here of late. Several horses have been stolen, and two Stiles boys who lived over in the neck and were deserters, were suspected. A company of men got after them, and caught one of them — the Youngest; he acknowledged to stealing and said his brother was concerned in it. They hung him on the same limb Stanley was hung on — so we have heard. They tell various tales about the other. one is that they shot him and broke his arm but did not take him. Another, that they took him, and hated to shoot him while a prisoner, so they guarded him loosely so he would try to make his escape, which he did. and then they shot him and broke his arm but he made his escape

EDITORIAL NOTE: The account of violence explicitly includes hearsay (“so we have heard”) and alternative stories. These are the writer’s reports; no independent verification is implied. Text continues on scan page 71.

06-071 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 71

06-071-P01
Last Sunday morning a party of 8 or 10 men passed Mrs Campbells — aunt [[Bats?]] house — with one of the Stileses. Jim Floyd was one of the party. a young man who was at Mrs Campbells asked Floyd what they were going to do with him. He replied, they were going to take the left hand road with him. The same day Jennie Bell saw a party enter the big thicket at their wood yard. They either took him in there and hung him, or went in to hunt him, after he made his escape. One thing is very certain to my mind, that is if they ever captured him, he never made his escape. Perhaps they merited death, but I dread the consequences of this mob law. We know not, but this is the beginning of terrible times. I hate the thought that Brushy [[deleted: should]] should be the place of the beginning of such things. I would feel a great deal better if I knew that man was not hanging over in that thicket.

06-071-P02
Fayette has not yet returned; the last news from them, Bob was having the chills, and was trying to get a furlough to come with F.

EDITORIAL NOTE: Continuation of the letter begun on scan page 69. The writer distinguishes belief from knowledge in her account of the suspected hanging.
