06-072 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 72

06-072-P01
Henry and Ellen went up last wednesday, so we are once more by our selves. Henry helped Father to sow his grass seed before he went away. They have got the stove set up, but the weather is pretty cool now to be without a fire place. They got the top of the well, a good many old shoes and large rocks and a Young possum out of the well.

06-072-P02
Tomorrow, brother Methodist Jones, will hold forth at Liberty. I will try to attend as I have not heard a discourse since the 1st of March.

06-072-P03
Joda says tell you he is at home safe, and a plowing. We will try to keep him busy while he is at home. There are so many of us here now that we are almost obliged to have some more room. We recd two letters from [[Fran?]], last dated 26th of March. He is very anxious to come home, but thinks it uncertain about getting to come. There is one thing I would like very much to have, a pair of Scissors if you can get them cheap, but do not inconvenience yourself about them.

06-072-P04
With best love to Warren and yourself I remain Lizzie

EDITORIAL NOTE: Final page of the letter begun on scan page 69.

06-074 | manuscript

06-074-P01
old nag, that used to be has fattened up, and looks like a young filly; She can pace like a streak, and is I might say the “star nag of the neighborhood”. Jeppe bought Laura’s pony when he went to San Antonio I consider him a poor horse he can walk pretty well when he is coming towards home but going from it, as May says about Joe, you have to work your passage. I wrote to you in my last about where we get water, Our well would be very good now if it was cleaned out, the rotten curbing spoils the taste. Our wheat looks fine if it was a little thicker it would be better.

06-074-P02
You invite criticism. Your composition I well might enjoy, if [[deleted: illegible]] were not expressly forbidden, but I would recommend a little more painstaking in your writing; so that the words can be more easily read. I know you know how to spell, but you sometimes leave out letters, which causes [[deleted: them]] [[inserted: words]] to be spelt wrong. I know you can soon remedy this, by a little carefulness in writing.

06-074-P03
I do not wish your letters shorter, but your writing might be a little compact, that is not stretched over so much paper, with out it was not such thick heavy paper, for your letters are sometimes marked “Due ten cents”. I must quit writing as it is getting late, and I expect you will be tired time you get all of this read.

06-074-P04
Yours Sister
Lizzie

06-074-P05
March 20th all well today Mr Graves Thos Uncle came over to work this morning they say Joe has got home he was taken prisner and Paroled by the Feds.

06-074-P06

Lizzie

judgement for that, but I think you ought to write a good letter to Sis, to make amends for the, allmost, rude word you sent her in Lizzies letter. 06-087 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 87

06-087-P01
[[Wednesday?]] night 15th This beautiful day has been passed very busily and pleasantly by me, we have been putting in a piece of cloth, and this evening [[Colburn?]] and Henry R. came over. [[Colburn?]] came to stay all night to go a turkey hunting with Joda in the morning, but was disappointed as Joda went to Dallas to day to see Watts having failed to see him at Ingeville as he went to Johnson; but they have let their plans to have a good hunt while Joda stays. You ought to be here to hear the turkeys gobble of a morning and see some of our beautiful pet deer. They are very gentle, since you have ceased to torment them. We also have another pet up in these western thickets, which [[illegible]] we suppose to be a panther. There are a kind of pets I would rather would be a little more distant. On the subject of John Turneys, the people of [[Tennessee?]] are anxious that he shall get a six mule team if he can, if not two four mule teams; They seem anxious to send plenty up there to the Co.

06-087-P02
Yours of March 30th we recd last Sunday. You say dont get too fast on the peace questions I will not, for I never was as sanguine as Father and [[Louisa?]] we all told them they were too fast. But I always have and always will think there is something wrong about this cotton trade. You have seen before this the account of the threatened invasion of Tex. from Ark. of twelve thousand men with a train of four hundred waggons. I rather reckon you have not seen the accounts of the wise disposal Genl McCullough has made of his men of late; or such items as these seldom go through the Telegraph. He appointed a day for them all to rendezvous at a certain place. Some of them being at home on furlough two hundred of them had arrived when they were surrounded by a Regmt and taken prisoners and then sent off to their respective commands where they had originally belonged, or to their enrolling officers. just turned out as I always expected it. un-fortunately a great many had not got there when the two hundred were captured. [[deleted: among them William Duff and his]] nephews Jno Smith who have returned to this section and report the [[illegible]]. They have joined Co. with [[illegible]]. This movement on the part of McCullough has rendered these men desperate. Rome has now gone to Wax. with a waggon master who he is acquainted with and who he thinks is his best friend, to try to get to drive a team from San Antonio to Eagle Pass. they say the Govt wants men who will bring their own teams. It will suit with R. if he can get into it but I cant help but think it is a trick to get him into the Service, though it may turn out that my fears are all groundless.

EDITORIAL NOTE: The weekday is tentative; it has not been changed to agree with the preceding page’s Thursday heading. Several personal names remain unresolved. The writer reports McCullough’s actions without independent verification.

06-089 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 89

06-089-P01
Brushy Nov. 13th 1864
Dear George

06-089-P02
You will have to forgive my delay, my excuse is busy times. This morning Lou and I went to meeting to Liberty to hear Mrs Justins funeral preached; it was to have been preached at the Colony last Sunday, but Mr Haynes had been sick and was not able to preach. To day the house was cramed jamed and running over. A goodmany of the Colony were up and a greatmany from about Shilo. Five preachers: Haynes, Justin, Maley, White, and Berry. Mr Haynes gave us a long tedious discourse from the last clause of the 4th vs. of the 11th ch. Hebrews. He spoke fully two hours, and a good portion of his sermon was about the depreciation of the currency (I am not talking about the “old” and “new issue”) but the money itself. The “old issue” was fully represented to day; and judging from the display have no idea of being repudiated soon. Em. and Liz. were there I just got to speak to them, had no time to talk. I wish you had been there to see all of the people. Times are about as they were when you were here; a good deal of work to do and not much time to visit. We assisted aunt Bettie in getting ready to move, she did not get her thread ready for me to weave, but got some jeans from Ellen, and we made the little boys coats. She got off on the morning of the 10th

====================================================================
06-090 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 90

06-090-P01
She took Betsy and chickens, left the spinnack and what few cows she had. I think [[her will intend?]] coming back to attend to them. Father bought a sow and five shoats from her for ten dollars specie, so I reckon we are safe in the meat line for another year. — that is if we can get corn raised to feed them on. Father has reserved twenty acres for [[corn?]] and hungarian. Laura wrote to you, Gibson was to fix the fence; she was mistaken that was not in the bargain; he was just to raise the wheat, cut it and thrash it and put a fourth in the granary. How the old fence is ever to be fixed up is more than I can tell but I hope it will all come out right. The well holds out finely about a dozen mules and horses are watered out of it twice and three times a day. It gets down pretty low, but hardly gets muddy, and by the next time is as full as ever. If I had any hopes of the war ending soon I would feel so much better but from the tone of the papers, we need not look for peace in four more years. Old Abe is elected in all probability and [[Childsy?]] thinks it is best for if Mc. Clelland is elected says he, propositions will be made which some of the States will accept, and then we are ruined. So it seems he is for four more years warfare.

06-090-P02
The wind was blowing cold from the north on last monday evening we were all sitting round the fire, when we heard [[coo a night owl?]] we looked out and sure enough there she was. [[Childsy?]] was nearly frozen, but a warm fire soon set him to talking, and he and Mr Grounds (a soldier that came with him) kept us laughing nearly all the time they were here.

EDITORIAL NOTE: The wording before “coming back” and the night-owl phrase remain tentative after review. The correspondent’s name appears to read Childsy; comparison with the physical original would help resolve its spelling.

06-091 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 91

06-091-P01
I was right glad to see [[Childsy?]], you and Joda had talked so much about him, that he seemed like an old acquaintance. I never saw any person more anxious to get home than he. I suppose Joda must have drawn you some shirts, by you sending that one home, draw every thing you can, for that is all the pay you can get. The shoes you sent Mr Grounds told us would not come up, he says they have been recalled.

06-091-P02
Henry and Ellen started home on the 8th, all well and hearty. we have not heard from Warren but look for him and Beck down. Griffin or somebody else must have a particular spite at Warren. If he did not get his furlough lengthened, his stay at home will be short. We recd a letter from Dock [[Wyrman?]] not long since, dated 10th Oct. He had just got Fathers letter and the socks we sent by Ed Dickson. Said he was very glad to get them as he needed them. He belongs to Co. G. Bates’ Reg which is stationed at Velasco, he says he expects they will remain there this winter. He winds up his letter by saying “I am a peace man, let it come as it may.” Ed Dickson is now at Tyler guarding prisoners. He deserves some credit for taking the trouble to send the socks over to Dock. Many a soldier, these scarce times would have kept them. [[Rome?]] it seems has a notion of hanging on to the service. The last letter Sister got from him he told her to buy two more waggons (a horse and an ox waggon) and come to him in the spring. Sister is mightily pleased, she has been wanting to leave here a long time. She thinks she can rent a house in San Antonio and send the children to school

EDITORIAL NOTE: The personal names Childsy and Wyrman remain tentative and are marked in the transcript.

06-092 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 92

06-092-P01
Wednesday [[eve?]] 16th When I commenced writing I expected to send this to the office before now, but it seems like a hard matter to get a letter to the office these days. I am perfectly furious this evening; at some future time I will let you know what it is about. If I can collect my senses sufficiently, I will try and fill this page. Jeppe got here yesterday he will only stay down a few days, as he is going to San Antonio with a load of wool as soon as he returns. Henry and Ellen [[inserted: had]] got back safe. Warren not got down; he can get no conveyance to bring Beck down while he stays; he wrote he would be here on the 10th of Dec. on his way back to Sabine. I suppose he got his furlough lengthened. On last monday Mr. Marshalls family [[deleted: was]] rolled in from Ark. composed of two sons and six daughters. They are greatly dissatisfied down on the old man for marrying, and wish they were back in Ark. they say they had plenty there of every thing and a good house to live in. The jayHawkers were the only ones that pestered them much. They say the feds treated them better than our folks. The old man has rented Lous house for them, he is going to build a chimney in it before they move there.

06-092-P02
As an other item of news I will tell you about [[Lidda?]] Bakers nose. Dr. Mc. [[Curdy?]] is making her a new one; he made a gold nose and then cut a piece of skin out of her forehead and twisted it around and covered the gold. when it knits at the sides he is going to cut it loose at the top and straighten it out: He says he is going to make her a lip when her nose gets well and then take her for pay. My paper is full and I will have to quit I reckon you are wishing I had. I cant send this to the office before Sunday. Affectionately Lizzie

06-092-P03
[[margin]] Jane Green died last Saturday night with pneumonia. [[/margin]]

06-092-P04
[[margin]] Aunt Bettie told me to tell you to write to her [[illegible]] Ark. [[/margin]]

EDITORIAL NOTE: Margin notes have been rotated and transcribed separately. The report of reconstruction of a nose and lip is historical correspondence, not medical advice or an independently verified case history.

06-094 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 94

06-094-P01
Brushy Texas No. 4
Feb. 19th 1864.
Brother George:

06-094-P02
Yours of the 7th and 8th came to hand this evening, and although short, gave us great satisfaction. When the Sundays mail fails to bring us any letters, as it did last Sunday, we are always uneasy until we do hear from you. Brushy has been in the receipt of various news to day; Laura got a letter from Fayette, M Orr one from Fayette, Mary and I one apiece from Jim.

06-094-P03
They have come safely through all the fights except the slight wound Jim rec. at Chickamauga. They both say they are tired of it. Jim says “he can stand the fatigue and hardships all very well, till it comes to the fighting fighting, and then when he sees his comrades falling around him, it is all he can do to persuade himself not to run.” Tole Hamilton fell at Ringold, in the retreat. he was shot through the breast, and only lived a few minutes. Poor fellow he has gone like many another from home and kindred, never more to return. He leaves a widow and child. The boys write home that two Goodloes (Aquilla’s sons) have deserted. And whose name do you expect to hear [[illegible]]? Tell no other than Squire. but uncle [[Givans?]] says this must [[illegible]] out of the family I know not why unless his boys wrote it home is the reason. But Squire is safe over the Border, where a many another poor Dixie boy wishes he was I expect. But Squire is not the only one of your acquaintance. The boys say that Charly Brown has written to them, that Will is safe with his kindred in a Northern Clime, hearty and well. Now dont you reckon that Will G. and Will Brown are having a happy time? Mary G. is perfectly satisfied about Billy. From the tone of Jodas letter, he seems to think your Batt. is disgraced worse than any other body of men in the Army, but he is behind the times, and had as well quit his noise. Tom [[Warthen?]] writes home that they have to keep a guard round Gurleys reg. to keep them from deserting. We hear there all of Murrays Texas Co. have left but Sixteen. I just give that at what it is worth. I am mistaken about Will G. being with

[[illegible]] are too highly colored [[illegible]] in all but the [[illegible]] but I think there are men in this Confederacy, mean enough to perpetrate any and all of the crimes committed by him, and that for less motive, than Quantrills men were actuated by. At least [[illegible]] were taking vengeance for [[illegible]]. But there are men in this confederacy who murder for money and I have some reason to believe, that [[Sherrol?]] is one of them. He tells of deeds he has committed that would make any honest person shudder — Such as going to a union man’s house, passing himself off for a union man get into the others Sentiments, then draw out his pistol and shoot him down. If all his tales are true, we have need to fear him.

06-097 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 97

06-097-P01
No. 13.
Brushy, June 28th 1864.
Dear Brother,

06-097-P02
I have sorrowful news to tell you this morning. Uncle Alfred is dead. Mr Orr recd a letter from Henry yesterday, confirming what we had heard before. He died late Sunday evening the 5th of this month, just 18 days after he was wounded. Poor Aunt Bettie is allmost crushed beneath the blow. As soon as she heard it was really true, she fainted away, and they thought for a while she would never revive again. Ever since, she has had cramping spells, and sometimes loses her breath. I have just returned from there, Mary Orr and I staid with her last night. I fear she will have a severe spell of sickness first — if she ever does get over it.

06-097-P03
He writes that the Drs made a good examination of his wound just a few days before he died, (which they had not been permitted to do previously, on account of the swelling) and discovered that the bone had been considerably fractured, just below the knee cap, and upon pressing it a good deal of matter run out with the oil of the knee cap.

06-098-P01
[[separate block: left-hand page]]
So you see it was a good deal worse, than was at first thought. Poor Uncle Alf. when he told Lou and me goodbye he said “I just want to get into one big fight, that will do me.” But now he sleeps between two comrades in arms — John Morgan, and [[deleted: illegible]] [[inserted: Docter]] Gardner. Bob [[Head?]] and his lady called upon us yesterday morning; he is rapidly recovering from his wound and will start to his command next week. He says Uncle A. was a good soldier — he watched him during the fight and he appeared to be the coolest man in the Co. Henry writes that cousin Gran is able to walk some, is in fine spirits, and will be able to start home in a few days. Bob has drawn a furlough and will come with Gran. Thos. Harper got in the other day, he says Parsons men have been ordered to Houston. By the by Who is Coz G’s correspondent.

06-098-P02
Well the Methodists are having another grand time at Liberty; their meeting commenced last Thursday night, and will probably end to night (tuesday). I have not attended except Sunday, there was so many people there that day, they had to move the seats out under the trees. I did not know half the people that were there. Parson Sansom preached in the morning, [[Coltr V.?]] in the evening. Had a splendid dinner on the ground and pretty girls were plenty, but thats no news to you.

06-098-P03
[[separate block: right-hand page]]
“But as in the days of Noah they shall marry and be given in marriage” Last Sunday night Old Mrs [[Liz?]] Hawkins and Old Mr Love were united in the bonds of matrimony; and not long since, Old man Durst and a widow Smith of Johnson Co; It is reported she has a hundred negroes. All I can say is [[illegible]] her. Since the old people have gone to marrying I suppose the young folks will think they have full licence now. I saw Sweet Sunday, it is reported that Jim has gone to the Feds, but I hardly think he would marry and go right straight off and leave his wife. I recd two letters from Fayette yesterday which Henry had recd and sent home. He appears to be in fine spirits says all the men over there are and that the most of them have reenlisted for 50 years or during the war. He says Jim is as lazy as he can be, just lolls around camp, and he accuses him sometimes of being in love, but cant tell how it is as he never gets any letters and he is sure he never sees any of the girls, unless it is in his sleep.

06-103 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 103

06-103-P01
Ann & Cynthia have just passed by going from aunt Catherines and say Warren Payne is dying he has been sick for some time. Lizzie and I will go up to sit up to night.

06-103-P02
Warren Payne is the youngest child of Preston Payne whom aunt [[inserted: Catherine]] took when he was only nine days old and has kept him ever since. Had he have lived seven more days he would be seventeen months old. His disease was chronic diarhea. Lynn Norman is at home on furlough; he has been out on the RioGrande for some months but is on his way to La. He has to report at Sabinetown on the eighth of Sept. He says the Reg. to which Eppa belongs has been ordered to Arizona. Another wildgoose chase like that of Sibleys Brigade. Gran Jetter is still at home. I dont think he is in much of a hurry to return to his command as he has been digging a well for two or three weeks past and intends to finish dig and [[wole?]] it with white rock before he thinks about fixing to go back. His furlough has been out a long time and he says when he is in the army all he thinks about is how to manage to get another. His patriotism has cooled down several degrees below zero. Mr. Chappell and his son Bob were in fifty miles of their home the last that has been heard of them. They are now violent secesh, but doubtless have got home and taken the oath to the feds long ago to keep out of the service in Dixie. Miss Ada Chappell is living at Gran Jettons and she is so patriotic that she says she had rather her father and brothers would all be killed than for this confederacy to go down. but she knows they are all out of danger now for Dock deserted and went home from La. last fall you can now form some idea of what kind of folks they are.
Sue

06-103-P03
[[margin]] I wont write any more while Lizzie is at home I am not well [[illegible]] [[/margin]]

August 21st A very pleasant morning. I sat up till two oclock last night. I do not understand the regulations. Now that Walker is in command, perhaps more men will be furloughed. The paper said Lt. Allen was shot on the 5th but as you said nothing about it in yours of the 7th I suppose it is not true. Mother and Sue have gone to Mr [[Grames?]] Father to Mr Grounds to take some socks to send to Cousin Dock by Ed. Laura did not come home so I am here by myself, except Ruth, sisters black girl whom we have hired for two or three months. I must go down and stay with May to day; and am in a hurry so excuse this botched up letter.

06-104-P03
I hope Sam will get your letter at the P O to day
Affectionately Yours Sister
Lizzie

06-109 | manuscript

06-109-P01
No. 16.
Brushy Texas, Sept. 6th 1864
Dear Brother

06-109-P02
I will again attempt to write. Last thursday I went up to the Office, and got yours of 10th ult. also the knife you sent by Mr. Collins, he passed a little while before I got to the office. I was sorry I did not get to see him. On yesterday I recd yours of 21st August; Lou, Jodas of the 25 which is the latest from you. I also received one from Frances Blair, who by the way is as good at letter writing, as conversation, and if you have ever been in her company much you know she is interesting. They were all well. Thomas has not yet gone into the service.

06-109-P03
Jeppe arrived this evening. He brings no news, left all well on Hack Creek though there is a good deal of sickness in the neighbourhood. Henry has had a gooddeal of practice of late; he has lost two patients, one of them was Daniel H. Willbanks, Warren [[deleted: knows]] [[inserted: knew]] him. Sallie Willbanks married Dr. Orr, and they have parted.

06-109-P04
Jeppe gathered his corn before he came down, made a hundred and thirty bushels. There has been several very sudden deaths in this section of late. Nancy Hancock, (Stout that was) was taken sick on Sunday and died on the next wednesday. They say after she died her eyes poped out of her head. While they were going to bury her, her little brother was taken suddenly, and died [[deleted: illegible]] in two hours he had had a chill or two before, but got up and helped to catch a chicken that morning and ate a hearty breakfast. It is a strange disease. They did not complain of any pain.

06-109-P05
[[margin]] Father has sold old Toby to Mr. Martin, for twenty three dollars in specie. [[/margin]]

====================================================================
06-110 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 110

06-110-P01
Mr. Telly — Nancy [[Culphers?]] husband — has been in bad health for a long time: on last Sunday morning a small red spot made its appearance on his arm, it spread rapidly and his arm swelled all over, mortification took place and he died on monday. I have heard of other sudden deaths, it is all thought to be the same disease, in different forms. J. Bell is now suffering severely from his wound. His shoulder is greatly swelled, and there is a place six inches long, in which he says he can feel the ball working back and forth. They think the ball will have to be cut out, but the Dr. thinks he had better not do it till the weather gets cooler.

06-110-P02
Wednesday evening, 7th At 10 oclock this morning, Mr. L. Evans called on us, and left the books you sent, also the letter from Joda. You better reckon we were glad to see some one from the boys. He came riding up through the rain, which had been falling nearly all morning; he was in such a hurry he did not stay more than fifteen minutes, we wanted him to stay for dinner but he was in such a hurry he did not want to [[deleted: illegible]] talk about it. I reckon thats the way they all are when they get pretty close home. He will get home to night, says he left Sabine on the 31st thats only seven days on the road. Dont you call that pretty good traveling.

06-110-P03
Mr Evans says your time will not come before Feb. he also says you could get leave of absence the same as he did. Now you know more about the matter than I do; but had you [[inserted: not]] better not come now, than wait till Feb, when perhaps the furlough business will all be stopped, or you sent off to La. or some where else? Thirty days is a short time for a man to come so far, he would not have long to stay at home but a short time is better than none. I am glad old Magruder is gone.

06-110-P04
[[margin]] The little baby is five months old and weighs nine pounds. We thank you for the book [[inserted: you sent me]] [[illegible]] [[/margin]]

06-110-P05
[[margin]] [[illegible]] says tell uncle [[illegible]] to see him [[/margin]]

06-111 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 111

06-111-P01
I will now have to tell you something more about the meetings which seem to be the most engrossing events of the day up this way. Every one preaches his own doctrine with all his might, consequently discussion [[inserted: is]] [[deleted: illegible]] rife. You wondered in one of yours, whether the good feeling which seemed to exist between different parties would continue to exist. I will give you a few items and you can draw your own conclusions: At our meeting in August it was announced that at the next appointment the meeting would be protracted, several eminent preachers were expected, and of course good preaching anticipated. Now this was not exactly in accordance with the feelings of our Methodist friends, so after studying on it awhile they appointed a meeting in Mr. [[Batakers?]] neighborhood embracing the same time! Not exactly as warm towards us as of yore, think you? The saying of Parson Hill, “now if me and my neighbor dont agree I just let him alone, dont go about him,” had sunk deep in their hearts, so union meetings were forgotten. Our meeting commenced at the appointed time, thursday night before the 1st Sunday. the congregations were small untill Sunday when, notwithstanding their efforts at “hedging” there were so many there, they had to move the seats out and preach under the grove. It is only the methodists that are so hostile. the presbyterians are not so afraid of the truth.

06-111-P02
Bro Jones was [[inserted: the]] only preacher from a distance, he did most of the preaching. By request Parson Austin addressed the people Sunday evening, [[inserted: he preached]] from the words “It is finished”. He made a very good talk. There were no conversions, but four united with the church — Milton Rawlins, his wife, Sarah Selby, and Lizzie [[Burnard?]]. The meeting closed on monday night at which time Bro Jones preached one of the best sermons I ever heard on the unity of the church. The house was full and I hope a good

EDITORIAL NOTE: Sectarian opinions are the writer’s own. Uncertain personal names remain marked in the text.

06-112 | manuscript
Source: ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf | PDF scan page 112

06-112-P01
impression made. Parson Austin was not a regular attendant but heard several good sermons. In his own language he says “I tell my brethren that they (the christians) have the bulge on us and we had better let them alone”. This is an odd expression. I dont know why it was done, but an other protracted meeting was appointed for the [[deleted: illegible]] beginning friday night before the first Sunday in Oct. Bro Goodman told us several preachers would be there we had never heard of before. How I wish you could be with us at that time. I know you would enjoy yourself.

06-112-P02
Parson Austin was [[deleted: illegible]] waiting on Lis. Gibbs last Sunday, and [[gave?]] Saturday night. Dont you think that is right early? his wife has only been dead a little over two weeks. Father has just returned from the widow Mc Knights; he went down to get an auger to bore for water. He brings two items of news; one is that Fayett and Jim have either been taken prisoners or killed. A letter has been recd stating the whole Brigade was captured. F. and J. have a hard time of it. The other item is, that Mary Morgan is to be married to night to Mr. [[deleted: illegible]] Phipp. And so they go. You wish to know if I would not like to partake of your cobler. It would be a treat indeed. I have only tasted one this year. But I would gladly take supper with you some night, if it consisted of bread and water only. I saw Walk Hatter the other day, he looks about as he used to. He was a prisoner four months, and says he fared well while in N’s hands. He says the Soldiers he talked with sayed the would fight us ten years but what they would whip us; that old Abe gave them plenty to eat and wear, and that is all they care for. You see I can write you long letters now; I have

Well [[illegible]] last monday was election day. The result is this, Frank Hawkins was elected again for city clerk. His opponents was named Motes Mr Love, (Bay's Father and the same one Mal. married). [[Bib?]] Hawkins, [[Tom?]] [[Coltham?]] and [[Mr?]] [[illegible]] were the candidates for [[Circuit?]] Clerk and [[illegible]] was the one who I wanted to vote. You know [[illegible]] was for Mr [[illegible]] But, as usual, my wishes were not consulted, and Mr. was beaten. He only came out about a week before the election & got only ninety votes. Love 130, Coltham 159. [[Norvell?]] will now live [[illegible]], but he already holds two offices—enrolling officer, and general of the militia. Abb [[illegible]] was taken under [[illegible]] and [[illegible]], now [[illegible]] the militia again, although uncle he was 50 last Sep and was given all his time in the last month. When [[illegible]] was enrolled before he protested against serving any longer than till he was fifty. He now says, he is going to stay at home till [[illegible]] after him, and then he is going to sue for false imprisonment, and he will carry it to the Supreme court, but what he will have his rights. They tried to get uncle Jim to go on with him, but he [[illegible]] submits as usual. I am sleepy so goodnight Em Gibbs staid with us last night I wish you had

We have no [[reliable?]] news from La.
[[cant?]] hear a word from [[Thad Co.?]]
althoug it will soon be a month
Since the battle. It is rumour
[[lost: torn edge]] here that we have whipt Steel
in Ars and captured two hundd
waggons. Seven men were recent
ly hung near Kentuckey Town
[[lost: torn edge]] one of these named [[Chisol?]] Mrs
[[lost: torn edge]] went to [[illegible]] the Guardian
(the man that raised her husba[[lost: torn edge]])
[[lost: torn edge]] to go with her to see if it was
[[lost: torn edge]] him. he would not go. said
[[lost: torn edge]] they ought to hang him if
[[lost: torn edge]] he want so see about [[illegible]]
[[lost: torn edge]] Sister Lizzie
